<meta>This page serves as the half-title page, formally introducing the main subject of the manuscript. It follows the sequence of blank flyleaves used to protect the interior text.</meta>

## THE ALCHEMICAL WRITINGS OF
## EDWARD KELLY.

<term>Edward Kelly</term> <note>Edward Kelly (1555–1597/8), also spelled Kelley, was a prominent and controversial figure in the English Renaissance. He was an alchemist and a self-declared spirit medium who famously worked alongside the royal advisor John Dee. Kelly claimed to possess the secret of transmuting base metals into gold using a mysterious red powder, and he remains a central figure in the history of Western occultism.</note>

<term>Alchemy</term> <note>A philosophical and protoscientific tradition practiced throughout Europe, Egypt, and Asia. It aimed to purify, mature, and perfect certain objects, most notably through the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone and the elixir of life.</note>

<summary>This page acts as the half-title for a collection of works by Edward Kelly, a renowned 16th-century alchemist and associate of John Dee.</summary>
<keywords>Edward Kelly, alchemy, alchemical writings, transmutation, Renaissance occultism</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the blank flyleaf and marks the beginning of the introductory biography of Edward Kelly. It sets a skeptical tone regarding Kelly's historical reputation compared to his own self-perception.</meta>

# BIOGRAPHICAL PREFACE.

---

“I VENTURE to hope,” says the subject of this memoir, in his treatise entitled *On the Philosopher's Stone* <note>original: "De Lapide Philosophorum"</note>, “that my life and character will so become known to posterity that I may be counted among those who have suffered much for the sake of truth.” The justification thus modestly desired by Edward Kelly has not been accorded him by the supreme court of judgment to which he appealed. Posterity continues to regard him in much the same light as he was looked at by the men of his immediate period, as a fraudulent notary who was deservedly deprived of his ears <note>It was widely rumored that Kelly’s ears were "cropped" or cut off as a legal punishment for forgery in his youth, though some biographers suggest he simply wore a black skullcap to hide them for other reasons</note>; as a sordid impostor, who duped the immeasurable credulity of the learned Doctor Dee <note>John Dee (1527–1608/9) was a brilliant Renaissance mathematician, navigator, and advisor to Queen Elizabeth I, whose reputation was later tarnished by his association with Kelly and occultism</note>, and subsequently involved his victim in transactions which have permanently

<summary>This page introduces the biographical sketch of the alchemist Edward Kelly, contrasting his own hope for a noble legacy with his historical reputation as a fraud and impostor.</summary>
<keywords>Edward Kelly, Doctor Dee, alchemy, philosopher's stone, biographical preface, history of science, imposture, Renaissance occultism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Edward Kelly’s negative reputation, specifically how his association with Dr. Dee has been characterized by historians and satirists. It bridges the description of Kelly as a "sordid impostor" to the broader historical bias against occultists.</meta>

<page-num>x.</page-num>

<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

degraded an otherwise great name; finally, as a pretended transmuter of metals <note>An alchemist who claims to turn lead or base metals into gold</note>, who was only too leniently treated by the emperor whom he deceived <note>Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, who patronized Kelly in Prague and later imprisoned him when the promised gold was not produced</note>. For example, the astrologer depicted by *Hudibras* <note>*Hudibras* is a famous 17th-century satirical poem by Samuel Butler that mocked the religious and intellectual pretensions of the era</note> had read

> “Dee's prefaces before
> The Devil, and Euclid o'er and o'er, <note>John Dee wrote a highly influential mathematical preface to the first English translation of Euclid’s *Elements* in 1570</note>
> And all th’ intrigues 'twixt him and Kelly,
> Laski <note>Albert Łaski, a Polish nobleman who brought Dee and Kelly to Europe</note> and th' Emperour would tell ye.”

But to Doctor Dee, at least, this is demonstrably unjust. This is the verdict of posterity in so far as it has concerned itself with the subject; it is the verdict of the biographical dictionaries, who have faithfully transcribed from one another, after the easy method which prevails with biographical dictionaries when they deal with magicians and seers, with alchemists and other professors of <term>mystic physics<gloss>The study of natural phenomena through spiritual or occult frameworks, common before the modern separation of science and religion</gloss></term>, as, generally, with all the oracles of the borderland; and, in so far as it concerns itself with borderlands, unerudite public opinion has been led by the erudite ignorance of the dictionaries.

<summary>This page critiques the historical dismissal of Dr. John Dee and Edward Kelly, arguing that biographical records have unfairly labeled them as frauds based on satirical portrayals and lazy scholarship. It highlights the influence of Samuel Butler's *Hudibras* in shaping this negative public perception.</summary>

<keywords>Edward Kelly, John Dee, Hudibras, Samuel Butler, Albert Łaski, Rudolf II, alchemy, mystic physics, historiography, occultism</keywords>

<page-num>xi.</page-num>

<header>Biographical Preface.</header>

Now, in offering for the first time to the English reader the three very curious treatises which constitute the chief literary remains of Edward Kelly, it is not necessary, as it would be in fact without reason, that the editor should accept an indiscriminate brief for the defence <note>A legal metaphor meaning a pre-prepared argument to protect someone's reputation regardless of the facts</note> of the alchemist who wrote them. To the collector of curiosities in science and unheard-of things <note>original: "choses inouies"</note> in literature, the interest which may attach to them will be unimpaired by the mummeries or crimes of their author. For the student of <term>Hermetic antiquities<gloss>The study of ancient alchemy, magic, and esoteric philosophy attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus</gloss></term>, it will become evident, and he may already be aware, that the value of the two treatises <note>original: "duo tractatus"</note> and their complement is not that they are the work of an <term>adept<gloss>A master alchemist who has achieved the Great Work and discovered the secret of transmutation</gloss></term>, but that they comprehend a careful digest or consensus of alchemical philosophers, while the interest which attaches to the man is created by his possession for a period of the two <term>tinctures<gloss>The 'White Tincture' and the 'Red Tincture,' alchemical substances believed to transform base metals into silver and gold, respectively</gloss></term> of alchemical philosophy, and not in his ability to compose them. At the same time, the adventures and imprisonments of Kelly, with

<summary>The editor introduces Edward Kelly's three treatises, arguing that their value lies in their comprehensive summary of alchemical philosophy rather than Kelly's personal character or mastery of the craft. It emphasizes that the texts are valuable to historians and collectors regardless of the author's controversial reputation.</summary>

<keywords>Edward Kelly, Hermetic antiquities, alchemy, two treatises, tinctures, adept, biographical preface, history of science</keywords>

<page-num>xii</page-num>

->*Edward Kelly :*<-

his transitions from abject poverty to sudden wealth, from a proscribed and law-hunted fugitive to a baron or marshal of Bohemia <note>Kelly was granted the title of 'Baron of the Kingdom' by Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II in Prague</note>, and then again to disgrace and imprisonment, ending in a death of violence, to say nothing of his visions and transmutations <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver</note>, constitute an astonishing narrative, and make up the broad outlines of a life which would be possible alone in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries <note>The author likely groups Kelly with the era of famous occultists and adventurers like Cagliostro, though Kelly actually lived in the 16th century (1555–1597)</note>. Moreover, here, as in so many other cases, the student of transcendental history <note>The history of the occult, supernatural, and esoteric movements</note> will hardly need to be informed that the “<term>skryer<gloss>A medium or crystal-gazer who 'sees' spirits or visions in a reflective surface like a crystal ball or mirror</gloss></term>” of <term>Doctor Dee<gloss>John Dee (1527–1608/9), a renowned English mathematician, astronomer, and occultist who served as a consultant to Queen Elizabeth I</gloss></term> and the discoverer of the so-called “Book of Saint Dunstan” <note>A legendary alchemical manuscript Kelly claimed to have found at Glastonbury Abbey, which supposedly contained the secret of the Philosopher's Stone</note> has been accredited with many iniquities of which he does not seem to have been guilty.

If it be permissible to set aside for the moment the mere antiquarian interest in these remains of <term>Edward Kelly<gloss>An English alchemist and spirit medium known for his collaboration with John Dee and his claims of achieving the Great Work</gloss></term>, and to exhibit a preferential attention towards that point of view from which the <term>Hermetic<gloss>Relating to alchemy and the esoteric philosophy derived from the legendary Hermes Trismegistus</gloss></term> student would be disposed to regard them, it will be reason-

<page-num>xiii.</page-num>

# Biographical Preface.

able to affirm that the importance of this alchemist's history concentrates entirely in his possession of the transmuting powders <note>The legendary "projection powders" or the Philosopher's Stone, believed to facilitate the alchemical transformation of base metals into gold</note>, and in the manner by which he is said to have acquired them. The other episodes of his life may be treated with comparative brevity.

<margin>* *Oxford’s Athens* <note>original Latin: *Athenæ Oxoniensis*</note>, ed. 1813, pp. 639-643.</margin>
Edward Kelly appears to have been born at Worcester, the event occurring, according to Anthony à Wood <note>A renowned 17th-century English antiquarian and historian (1632–1695) who documented the lives of Oxford graduates</note>,* about four o'clock in the afternoon on the first day of August, 1555. This was in the third year of Queen Mary's reign <note>Mary I, the Tudor queen who reigned from 1553 to 1558</note>. He was educated in his native city until the age of seventeen, when he is supposed to have repaired to Oxford. The registers of that University contain no record of any Edward Kelly having entered at the period in question, and it is assumed that his real name was Talbot. Three persons bearing this designation were entered at <term>Gloucester Hall<gloss>A former academic hall of the University of Oxford, which later became Worcester College</gloss></term> about this time. Possibly the University records have not been adequately searched, and

<summary>This page details the early life and birth of the alchemist Edward Kelly in Worcester, noting the historical ambiguity regarding his enrollment at Oxford University under the name Talbot.</summary>

<keywords>Edward Kelly, Worcester, Oxford University, transmuting powders, Anthony à Wood, Gloucester Hall, Queen Mary I, Thomas Talbot, alchemy biography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into Edward Kelly's early adulthood and the conflicting accounts of his education. It follows the previous page's discussion regarding whether Kelly attended Oxford University under the pseudonym "Talbot."</meta>

->Edward Kelly :<-

if not, the evidence for his stay at Oxford is of a very weak character.* If, besides the difficulty that has been mentioned, there are no other reasons for supposing that he changed his name, and none others seem to be found, there is, perhaps, more reason to doubt his university career than to accept the theory of the <term>alias<gloss>An assumed name or identity</gloss></term>. If he were at Oxford, it would be only for a short period, and he is said to have left abruptly. Other accounts state that he was trained as an <term>apothecary<gloss>A historical term for a medical professional who prepared and sold drugs and medicines; a precursor to the modern pharmacist</gloss></term>, and in this way acquired some skill in <term>chemistry<gloss>In the 16th century, this often referred to alchemy or early experimental chemical science</gloss></term>. It was more probably the profession of his father, of which he may have picked up some knowledge in boyhood. After the end of his formal studies, whether at Oxford or elsewhere, he himself seems to have pursued the law, and to have settled in London, or, according to another account, at Lancaster, but possibly in both places. ->Biographical Preface.<-

It was in the latter <note>referring to Lancaster, mentioned at the end of the previous page</note> certainly that his troubles began. He was a <term>skilful penman<gloss>A person with great talent in handwriting and calligraphy</gloss></term>, who had gone to great effort to familiarize himself with archaic English, and, as a <term>Worcester<gloss>A cathedral city in the West Midlands of England</gloss></term> man, it is not unlikely he was acquainted with Welsh. By the help of these accomplishments, he was accused of producing <term>forged title deeds<gloss>Fraudulent legal documents created to falsely claim ownership of land or property</gloss></term> in the interests of a client.

The <term>indictment<gloss>A formal legal charge or accusation of a serious crime</gloss></term> is very vague and does not rest upon anything that can be termed solid evidence. He is said, however—though upon equally uncertain grounds—to have been <term>pilloried<gloss>A form of public punishment and humiliation where the offender is secured in a wooden framework to be mocked or pelted by the public</gloss></term> at Lancaster, and to have been also <term>deprived of his ears<gloss>A historical punishment known as "cropping," often used for forgery or spreading sedition</gloss></term>.

There is no doubt that he fell into grievous trouble, for to his life's end he was always more or less in fear of English law, and sometimes seems to have preferred a foreign prison to the uncertain reception which was to be anticipated on his return home. But that the penalty which his biographers have meted out to him, whether deserved or not by his misdeeds, was in some way evaded, it seems more reasonable to think. The...

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Edward Kelly's legal troubles in England and the physical punishments he allegedly suffered, specifically the cropping of his ears as a penalty for forgery. It argues against the likelihood of this punishment having occurred and transitions to his flight to Wales and his use of aliases.</meta>

<page-num>xvi.</page-num>
<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

The prominent position which he held subsequently at the Court of the Emperor Rudolf <note>Rudolf II (1552–1612), the Holy Roman Emperor who was a famous patron of alchemy and the occult</note> would have scarcely been possible for a man who had lost his ears. The gullibility of royal personages at the end of the sixteenth century <note>The original text says "seventeenth century," likely a chronological error by the author, as Kelly's career took place in the late 1500s</note> may have facilitated many frauds on the part of the alchemists whom they protected, but could scarcely have extended to accepting the philosophical wisdom of an <term>adept<gloss>A master of alchemical secrets who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of transmutation</gloss></term> who had been physically branded by the law as a criminal. 

The alternative story is, then, apparently preferable, and this says that Kelly sought refuge in Wales. Here it is exceedingly probable that he adopted an assumed name, but whether Talbot <note>Edward Kelly was known to use the alias "Edward Talbot" when he first approached the scholar John Dee in 1582</note> became Kelly or whether Kelly merged for a moment into Talbot, or some other designation, is a mystery of identity as complex as a transformation in <term>Alchemy<gloss>The ancient practice of attempting to turn base metals into gold and find a universal elixir</gloss></term> which the past is not likely to reveal. In Wales he would seem to have embraced a wandering life, staying at obscure inns, and after a time he must have worked his way down into the neighborhood of the historic abbey of—

<summary>The author argues that Kelly likely avoided the physical punishment of having his ears cropped, as such a brand would have barred him from the imperial court. It describes his subsequent flight to Wales and his use of the alias Talbot.</summary>

<keywords>Edward Kelly, Emperor Rudolf II, alchemy, adept, Wales, Edward Talbot, legal punishment, aliases</keywords>

that it treated of the <term>transmutation</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals, like lead or copper, into precious metals such as gold or silver.</note> of metals. He made inquiries as to the history of this bibliographical rarity, and learned that its discovery was due to one of those outbursts of religious fanaticism <note>Refers to the iconoclasm of the English Reformation, where church monuments were often destroyed by those seeking to purge "popish" or superstitious elements.</note> which were common enough in the reign of Queen Elizabeth. The sepulchre of a departed bishop interred in a neighbouring church had been violated, the zeal of the Protestant being not unmixed with the desire of obtaining hidden treasures. However, the sacrilegious act was only rewarded by the <term>alchemical</term> manuscript which the despoilers could not read, and by two small ivory caskets, containing respectively a red and a white powder <note>In the alchemical tradition, the "Red Work" (rubedo) and "White Work" (albedo) refer to the final stages of creating the Philosopher’s Stone, used to create gold and silver respectively.</note>, which in their eyes were equally useless. The receptacle of the red powder was shattered in their fury, and much of its contents were lost. What remained of it, together with the second casket and the accompanying document, they readily disposed of to the innkeeper, who seems to have had a hand in the discreditable transaction, in exchange for a flagon of wine. <page-num>xix.</page-num>
->### Biographical Preface. xix.<-

The manuscript was kept as a curiosity to be shown to strangers visiting the inn; the undamaged ivory casket became a toy for the innkeeper’s children; a remnant of the red powder happened to remain in its broken container; and it so happened, in time, that Kelly, in his role as a traveler, examined the entire find. If Kelly had started his life as an apothecary <note>A person who prepared and sold medicines; a precursor to the modern pharmacist.</note>, he undoubtedly had a basic knowledge of chemistry, and there would have been few educated people at that time who, regarding Alchemy, had heard nothing of the red and white tinctures <note>The "red tincture" was believed to turn metals into gold, while the "white tincture" turned them into silver.</note> which were the tools of the **Great Work** <note>original: "Magnum Opus"; the ultimate goal of alchemy, which was the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. He knew enough to be eager to own them, and for the entire collection of ancient items, he offered one guinea <note>A gold coin worth 21 shillings; a substantial sum for an innkeeper to receive for "useless" items.</note> to the innkeeper, who accepted the offer.

->xxv.<-

# Biographical Preface.

Whatever has been advanced to the contrary by sensational biographers like **Louis Figuier** <note>Guillaume Louis Figuier (1819–1894) was a prolific French writer who popularized science and history, though he was often criticized for embellishing facts for dramatic effect.</note>, elaborating with a view to effect, it was not in the main as an <term>alchemist</term> <note>A practitioner of alchemy, the medieval art aimed at transmuting base metals into gold and discovering a universal elixir.</note>, but as a <term>seer in the crystal</term> <note>Also known as a "scryer"; a person who uses a reflective object, such as a crystal ball, to perceive visions or communicate with spirits.</note> that **Edward Kelly** posed before the doctor of **Mortlake** <note>The "doctor of Mortlake" refers to the famous scholar and occultist John Dee (1527–1608/9), who lived in Mortlake, London.</note>. It was also in that capacity that he chiefly influenced his companion. 

It is immaterial for the purposes of this notice—which, as already intimated, is not devised as an apology for its subject—to determine whether the visions of Edward Kelly were genuine or not. In the present state of psychological knowledge, imperfect as it still is, it is, on the one hand, too late to deny that a state of <term>lucidity</term> <note>A term used in early psychology and spiritualism to describe a state of heightened clarity or a visionary trance.</note> can be frequently induced by the mediation of crystals and similar transparent substances; while it is evident, on the other hand, from the history of the subject, that beyond the bare fact and such possibilities as may be reasonably attached to it, nothing of real moment has resulted from any such experiments. 

Edward Kelly may have lost his ears <note>This refers to the historical rumor that Kelly had his ears cropped as a judicial punishment for forgery or counterfeiting in his youth, a brand of shame he allegedly hid with a black skullcap.</note>

# Edward Kelly :

On the 21st of September, 1583, Edward Kelly and his patron <note>Dr. John Dee, the famous mathematician and occultist who served as an advisor to Queen Elizabeth I.</note> left England for the continent. Various reasons have been assigned for this removal, as, for example, that Kelly went in continual fear of his liberty and even his life; that they could not carry on their

---

the body but the evocation <note>The act of calling upon or summoning a spirit or deity.</note> of the spirit of the deceased), and to answer to such questions that he then proposed to him." An original letter to Wood signed "**Anonymous Philomusus**" <note>A pseudonym meaning "Anonymous Lover of the Muses," often used by scholars of the era.</note>, and preserved among the **Tanner Manuscripts** <note>A collection of historical documents and letters held at the Bodleian Library.</note> in the **Bodleian Library** <note>The main research library of the University of Oxford.</note>, says that **Weever's** <note>John Weever (1576–1632), an English antiquary and poet known for recording ancient funeral monuments.</note> authority was an accomplice of Kelly at the time of this transaction. As all species of magic were then vulgarly regarded as of Satanic origin, it is obvious, of course, that, from this standpoint, Kelly had commerce with evil spirits. In this connection there is one interesting citation from the "*Diary of Doctor Dee*." "April 13, 1584, *around 3 o'clock* <note>original: circa, 3 horam</note>. After a short request made by me to Christ for wisdom and verity to be ministered by **Nalvage** <note>One of the "Enochian" spirits or angels that Kelly claimed to communicate with through a crystal or "shew-stone."</note> (*that is* <note>original: i.e.</note>, one of the spirits of the crystal), he appeared and spake much to E. K., which he expressed not to me, but at length confessed that he gave him brotherly counsel to leave dealing as an idolater or fornicator against God, by asking counsel of such as he did." Thereupon "E. K. confessed that he had been dealing with the devil." ->xxix.<-

<header>Biographical Preface.</header>

<term>alchemical</term> <note>Alchemy was the medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transmutation of matter, particularly turning base metals into gold.</note> experiments under the best circumstances in their own country; that such operations were calculated to make them notorious, and liable to the superstitious fury of the populace; that Doctor Dee, in particular, had been disappointed of reasonably expected preferment. All these factors brought Kelly

---

into unison with him (as the Doctor himself declares in the preface to his work on the ministration of spirits <note>This likely refers to Dee's records of spiritual "actions" or his book *De Heptarchia Mystica*, which detailed the hierarchy of spirits he believed he was contacting.</note>) by mediation of the angel **Uriel** <note>In Judeo-Christian tradition, Uriel is an archangel; Dee believed Uriel provided him with his "shew-stone" or crystal ball for communicating with the spirit world.</note>. But Doctor Dee was undoubtedly deceived in his opinion that the spirits which ministered to him were executing the Divine will, and were the messengers and servants of the Deity. Throughout his writings on this subject, he evidently considers them in this light; which is still more indisputably confirmed by the piety and devotion he invariably observed at all times when these spirits had intercourse with him. And further, when he found his coadjutor Kelly was degenerating into the lowest and worst species of the <term>magic art</term> <note>At this time, "learned magic" was often distinguished from "low magic," though both were viewed with extreme suspicion by the church and state.</note>, for the purposes of fraud and avaricious gain, he broke off all manner of connection with him, and would never be seen in his company. But it is believed the doctor, a little before his death, became sensible that he had been imposed upon by these invisible agents, and that all their pretence of acting under the auspices of the angel Uriel, and for the honour and glory of God, was but mere hypocrisy and the delusion of the devil. Kelly, being thus rejected and discountenanced by the Doctor, betook himself to the most mean and vile practices of the <term>magic art</term>; in all of which pursuits money and the works of the devil appear to have been his chief aim. Many wicked and abominable transactions are recorded of him, which were performed by <term>witchcraft</term> <note>Commonly understood then as malevolent magic involving a pact with evil spirits.</note> and the mediation of infernal spirits; but nothing more fitting <note>original: "apropos"</note> to the present subject—

## II.

### THE BOOK OF ST. DUNSTAN.

The student of **alchemical** literature will naturally be curious to know whether the mysterious manuscript of Glastonbury has been pretended to have survived. Tradition has ascribed to it the name which heads this section, and there is the following evidence, which must be taken <note>The phrase "taken with all faults" is a legalistic way of saying "accepted despite potential inaccuracies."</note> with all faults, to account for it. The abbey of Glastonbury was founded by **Saint Dunstan** <note>Saint Dunstan (909–988 AD) was an Abbot of Glastonbury and Archbishop of Canterbury. He was the patron saint of goldsmiths and metalworkers, which led later alchemists to claim him as one of their own.</note>, but he does not appear to have been buried there, despite the supposed <term>translation</term> <note>In a religious context, "translation" refers to the ceremonial moving of a saint's body or relics from one location to another.</note> of his relics from Canterbury. Yet it must be inferred from the tradition that the remains of the disinterred bishop were those of the saint himself. Saint Dunstan was supposed to

In alchemical literature, an "ark" or "chest" often refers to a repository of hidden, sacred knowledge.</note>, which is followed by the *Treatise of the Great Lord Dunstan, Bishop of Canterbury and true philosopher, regarding the Philosopher’s Stone* <note>Original: *Tractatus Maximi Domini Dunstani, Episcopi Cantuariensis, veri philosophi, de Lapide Philosophico*. While Dunstan was a real 10th-century archbishop, these alchemical works were attributed to him centuries later by those wishing to lend authority to their texts.</note>. Several surviving manuscripts, in both Latin and English, which differ greatly in their dates and the nature of their contents, are nonetheless attributed to St. Dunstan. The first printed edition was published at Cassel in 1649. A few uneducated critics have gone so far as to claim that Kelly’s own <term>treatise</term> <note>A formal and systematic written discourse on a subject.</note> is the authentic Glastonbury manuscript itself. Others, dismissing the connection to the saint, have been inclined to consider two poetic works, which are included by Elias Ashmole in the *British Chemical Theatre* <note>Original: *Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum*. Published in 1652, this is a famous collection of English alchemical poetry compiled by the antiquarian Elias Ashmole.</note>, as being the original work, though obviously not in the original form or language. There may be little evidence to support this theory, but it is not impossible; if it is rejected, the verses in question may be safely regarded as additional literary remains of...

## SIR EDWARD KELLY’S WORK.

All you that would gladly be philosophers,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And night and day boil yourselves in <term>Geber’s</term> <note>Abu Musa Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a Persian polymath and alchemist whose works formed the foundation of medieval European alchemy.</note> kitchen,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Wasting the scraps of ancient <term>Hermes’ Tree</term> <note>A reference to Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy. The "tree" often symbolizes the growth of the alchemical process or the "Philosophic Tree" from which the Great Work blooms.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Thinking to turn them into a precious oil,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The more you work, the more you lose and spoil;
To you, I say, however learned you may be,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Go burn your books and come and learn from me.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Although you have read ten books for every one of mine,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That is not enough, for I have heard it said
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The greatest scholars are not the wisest men:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A lion once obeyed a lowly mouse <note>A reference to Aesop’s fable "The Lion and the Mouse," suggesting that even the powerful or learned can be aided by those they consider inferior.</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In my good will, consider yourselves satisfied,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And though I do not write half as sweetly as <term>Tully</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator renowned for his eloquent prose.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Yet you shall find that I follow the steps of <term>Lully</term> <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic whose logical systems were highly influential in later alchemical and occult thought.</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It does you good to think how your desire
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And self-conceit gives authority to empty hope;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You spare no expense, and you lack no coals for your fire,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You know the virtues of the <term>Heliotrope</term> <note>A green mineral with red spots (also called bloodstone) or a flower that turns to the sun. In alchemy, it was associated with solar energy and sometimes credited with the power to make its wearer invisible.</note>;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You think yourselves far richer than the Pope;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What thing exists, whether high or low,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But you claim to know its *first matter* <note>Original Latin: *materia prima*. In alchemical theory, this is the original, formless substance from which all things are created and to which they must be reduced before they can be transformed into gold.</note>.

<summary>This page features a poem attributed to Edward Kelly that mocks the vanity and futility of academic alchemists, urging them to abandon their books in favor of his practical wisdom. Kelly aligns himself with the esoteric traditions of Ramon Llull while dismissing the empty rhetoric of those who have not achieved the Great Work.</summary>

<page-num>xliv.</page-num>
<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

## The Elixir of Life <note>original: "Elixir vitæ"</note> and the Precious Stone

You think you know the way as well as how to grow an apple;
     But when it comes to the actual work, you are lost alone;
You think you know the colors: black, brown, reddish-brown, 
     and dapple;
If someone corrects you, then you begin to bluster <note>original: "fraple" – to quarrel or speak noisily and pointlessly.</note>,
Swearing and saying, "What kind of man is this?"
Yet still you work, but you always work in vain.

No, no, my friends, it is not boastful words,
     Nor mighty oaths that gain that sacred skill;
It is obtained by grace and not by swords,
     Nor by great reading, nor by sitting still,
Nor by foolish imagination, nor by working only by your own will,
But, as I said, by grace it is obtained;
Seek grace, therefore, and let your folly be restrained.

It is no costly thing, I assure you,
     That produces <term>Magnesia</term> <note>In alchemy, Magnesia is not the modern mineral, but a name for the "prime matter" or the secret starting material of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in her true form;
Yet she herself is made pure by <term>leprosy</term> <note>An alchemical term for the "impurities" or surface corrosion of a metal that must be removed through the process of "cleansing" or "killing" the metal.</note>,
     Her eyes are clearer after first being made blind,
And he who can first unbind the tightness of the earth
     Shall quickly know that I have told the truth
Of sweet <term>Magnesia</term>, the wife to the purest gold.

Now, what is meant by "man and wife" is this:
     The <term>Agent and Patient</term> <note>The active (masculine) and passive (feminine) principles that must be combined in the alchemical vessel.</note>, yet not two but one,
Just as Eve was Adam’s wife, I know <note>original: "I wisse"</note>,
     Flesh of his flesh and bone of his bone—
Such is the oneness of our precious <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy, capable of transmuting base metals and granting long life.</note>;
As Adam slept until his wife was created,
     Even so it is with our <term>Stone</term>; no more can be said.

<summary>In these verses, Edward Kelly explains that the alchemical "Stone" is achieved through divine grace rather than pride or study, using the biblical metaphor of Adam and Eve to describe the union of alchemical principles.</summary>
<keywords>Edward Kelly, Elixir of Life, Magnesia, alchemy, Agent and Patient, Philosopher's Stone, Adam and Eve, purification, spiritual alchemy</keywords>

By this you see how it came to pass
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That first was man, and then woman came from him;
Thus Adam here was the first and highest <note>original: "cheefest"</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And still remained a man of perfect form <note>original: "limme"</note>;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then man and wife were properly joined together <note>original: "joyned together trimme"</note>,
And each turned toward the other in love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And increased their kind once God had
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;blessed them.

In the same way, the man—our <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy, here personified as a masculine principle.</note>—is put to sleep,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;His newly-made spouse whom he bought so
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;dearly;

Rejoice in the beauty of such a fair bride,
Whose worth is more than half the world
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;beside.

I suspect that so far you hardly understand
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What "man" or "wife" truly signifies,
And yet I know you convince yourselves <note>original: "beare your selves in hand"</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That it is undoubtedly <term>Sulfur</term> and <term>Mercury</term>;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And so it is, but certainly not the common kind;
But <term>essential Mercury</term> <note>Also known as "Philosophical Mercury," this is not the liquid metal found in thermometers, but a purified "spirit" extracted from matter.</note> is truly the wife
That kills herself to bring her child to life. <note>A reference to the stage of "putrefaction," where the chemical components must "die" or decompose in the flask to be reborn as the Elixir.</note>

For first and foremost she receives the man,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Her perfect love soon makes her
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;conceive,
Then she strives with all the force she can,

In spite of her love, she robs him of his life,
Which being done, she will never rest,
But labors naturally like a loving wife
Until she has brought him back to life again.

Then he, in turn, to repay her kindness,
Places a crown of glory upon her head,
And composes poems to her praise—
Poems that inspire every poet to write a story;
And she is not sorry that she slew him then,
For by the virtue of his loving wife,
He not only lives, but also grants life to others.

But here I wish for you to rightly understand
How he here makes his <term>concubine</term> <note>In alchemy, a "concubine" often refers to a raw or "unmarried" substance that must be purified before it can be truly "wedded" into the Stone.</note> into his wife;
If you do not know how this is done, do not attempt
This work, which is not easily shared with fools.
And see that you bring peace <note>original: "attonement"</note> where there is conflict;

Then strip the man down to his shirt of fine silk <note>original: "tishew," referring to a rich, thin fabric.</note>,
And her out of her undergarment, to <term>ingender</term> <note>To procreate or produce.</note> offspring <note>original: "yssue"</note>.

To tell you the truth, he lacks for no wives,
In land or sea, in water, air, or fire;
Without their deaths, he does not value their lives.
Unless they live, he lacks his chief desire;
He binds them as an <term>prentice</term> <note>Apprentice.</note> to the most skillful dyer <note>The "dyer" refers to the alchemical process of "tincturing" or changing the color of the metal.</note>,
And once they have endured all their sorrows,
They find joys that were hidden from them at first.

For then they find the joy of sweet increase;
They bring forth children beautiful to the sight,

Those which are able to release prisoners,
And give true light to the darkest bodies,
Their heavenly <term>tincture</term> <note>A potent alchemical substance or "medicine" capable of transforming base metals into gold or curing disease; it represents the perfected state of the matter.</note> is of such great might;
Oh! He that can but happen upon such a treasure,
Who would not think his joys were beyond measure?

Now by this question I shall quickly know
If you can tell which is his wife indeed—
Is she fast-moving, fair-faced, yes or no?
<term>Flying</term> <note>Volatile; substances that easily evaporate or react when heated.</note> or <term>fixed</term> <note>Stable; substances that remain solid and resist the fire.</note>, as you read in books?
Is she to be fed, or does she do the feeding?
In what does she rejoice? Where is her home?
Heavenly, or earthly, or from a strange nation?

What is she? Poor? Or is she of any wealth?
Fine in her clothing, or humble in her apparel?
Or is she sick? Or is she in perfect health?
Mild in her nature? Or is she prone to quarrel?
Is she a glutton? Or does she love the barrel?
If you say she is any one of these things,
You do not know his wife, nor have you ever seen her.

And that I will prove to you by good reason,
That truly she is none of all these things;
This is a question for you that is <term>geason</term> <note>An archaic word meaning rare, uncommon, or extraordinary.</note>;
And yet, she must be some part of them all:
Why then, you may see that some part is not the whole.
Therefore, the true wife of whom I speak
Is the <term>mean between</term> <note>The "golden mean" or middle path; in alchemy, the Stone is often described as a perfect balance between opposing qualities like hot and cold or dry and moist.</note> all these opposites.

<summary>This page presents a poetic riddle concerning the "alchemical wife," challenging the reader to understand that the secret substance is a perfect balance of opposing physical properties.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s alchemical poem. Having previously discussed the "wife" as a "mean" or middle state, Kelly now uses a domestic metaphor—the making of bread—to explain the union of the primary alchemical principles.</meta>

<page-num>xlviii.</page-num>

# Edward Kelly:

As meal and water joined both together
  Are neither meal nor water now, but dough,
Which, being baked, is neither dough nor water:
  Nor will they ever separate from one another;
  The <term>mean</term> <note>The "mean" or "middle" refers to the mediating substance or state that joins two opposites into a new, inseparable third substance.</note> between them is the wife—our wife—even so,
And in this hidden point our secret lies—
It is enough; few words content the wise.

Now by this simile here I do reveal
  A mighty secret, if you mark it well;
Call <term>mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the feminine, volatile, and liquid principle of matter.</note> "water," and imagine <term>sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur represents the masculine, active, and combustible principle.</note> is "meal,"
  What "meal" I mean I hope the wise can tell;
  Bake them by <term>craft</term> <note>The "craft" refers to the Alchemical Art—the specific laboratory processes of heating and refining.</note>, make them together 
    dwell,
And in your working do not make too much haste,
For she is not the "wife" while she is still in paste.

This lesson learned, now give me leave to play,
  I shall be the better prepared to learn another,
My mind is turned <term>clean cam</term> <note>An archaic expression meaning "completely awry" or "in a totally different direction."</note> another way;
  I do not love to smother sweet secret 
    thoughts—
  It is a child, you know, that makes a mother;
Since it is so, then we must have a child,
Or else of motherhood we are <term>beguiled</term> <note>Cheated or deprived of the experience.</note>.

What will you say if I tell you a wonder,
  And prove the mother is both child and mother 
    too?
Do you not think I am trying to sell you
  A trick in sport, as some are known to do?
  Is it possible for the mother to wear her infant's 
    shoe?

<summary>Kelly uses the metaphor of mixing flour and water into dough to illustrate how alchemical Mercury and Sulphur combine into a new, inseparable substance. He concludes with a riddle about the "mother" and "child," hinting at the cyclical nature of the alchemical transformation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s alchemical poem. <page-num>xlix.</page-num>

# Biographical Preface.

->4 A<-

Truly, this is part of our philosophy,
As I will shortly demonstrate through logic.
<term>Ripley</term> <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was one of the most famous English alchemists. His writings were considered essential textbooks for later alchemists like Kelly.</note> advises you not to dismiss this,
    But with patience to wait for the true <term>conjunction</term> <note>The union of two opposite substances (often called "Sulphur" and "Mercury") into one.</note>;
For, says he, our child is born in the air,
    There he receives the holy <term>unction</term> <note>Anointing with oil. In alchemy, this refers to the substance receiving a spiritual or "oily" quality that allows it to resist fire.</note>,
    And with it, a heavenly purpose;
For after death, he is revived again to life—
He is all in all: husband, child, and wife.

While the matter is yet "earth," it is called "conception,"
    And <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The stage where the matter turns black and "dies" or decomposes before being purified.</note> is the time of "lying-in" <note>The period of rest for a woman after giving birth.</note>;
Perfect conjunction (as <term>artes-men</term> <note>Skilled practitioners of the Alchemical Art.</note> affirm)
    Is the woman’s "childing" <note>Giving birth.</note> where all joy begins.
    He who does not know this is very dim-witted;
When she is strong and shines fair and bright,
She is termed *the wife most beautiful to sight*.

Behold! Thus you see that you are not deceived!
    For, if you notice, I have proved by reason
How the mother and the child are both one,
    Through conception, breeding, and birthing in every season;
    I have explained this to you without any betrayal,
Or any false, ambiguous words at all;
I have shown you the work, and you shall find it true.

This is that <term>Mercury essential</term> <note>Also called "Philosophical Mercury." This is not the common liquid metal (quicksilver), but a refined, spiritualized substance that serves as the root of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in truth,
    Which is the principle of the material Stone,
And not those raw <term>amalgams</term> <note>Simple mixtures of mercury with other metals, which Kelly dismisses as "superficial" or fake alchemy.</note> recently begun—
    Those are but superficial mercuries.
    This is that <term>menstruum</term> <note>A universal solvent used to dissolve the "body" of the metals to reach their "soul." The term refers to the cycles of nature.</note> of perfect <term>tincture</term> <note>The power of the Stone to "dye" or transform base metals into gold.</note>;
This is most truly that "one thing"
From which all profit must spring.

<summary>In this passage, Edward Kelly explains the symbolic birth of the Philosopher's Stone, using metaphors of pregnancy and childbirth to describe the stages of chemical transformation. He distinguishes "Essential Mercury"—the true alchemical principle—from common chemical mixtures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical poem by Edward Kelly. Following the previous page’s discussion on the "conception" and "birth" of the alchemical child, Kelly now moves into the practical and cautionary advice for the practitioner, invoking the authority of George Ripley to describe the physical manipulation of the "Stone" and the transition from the black stage of putrefaction to the white stage of purification.</meta>

<page-num>1.</page-num>

# *Edward Kelly:*

If this does not satisfy you, then remain displeased with me,
  For I have finished. If reason cannot prevail,
What can be said, but that doubts will persist,
  No matter what one does, when folly wins the race;
  Let it be enough to say that this is the perfect base,
Which is the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> that must be dissolved:
How that is done, I will declare to you.

This is the <term>Stone</term> that <term>Ripley</term> <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist whose "Compound of Alchemy" was a primary source for later seekers like Kelly.</note> tells you to take
  (For until it reaches this state, it is not yet a stone):
Follow my guidance, do not ignore my advice;
  And he also commands: leave "crudities" <note>Raw, unrefined, or impure materials that have not yet been prepared by alchemical fire.</note> alone,
  If you have the grace to keep yourself free from grief.
Then stick to this path, and do not let <term>phansey</term> <note>Imagination or whimsical, undisciplined thought that leads an alchemist away from the true laws of nature.</note> overcome you;
Let reason rule, for imagination will betray you.

Take this <term>Stone</term>, this "wife," this "child," this "all,"
  Which will be gummy, crumbling, silken, and soft;
Grind it into small pieces upon a plate of glass or <term>porphire</term> <note>Porphyry, a very hard igneous rock used for grinding slabs to ensure the alchemical materials were not contaminated by the stone of the mortar itself.</note>,
  And, as you grind, feed it frequently with <term>Mercury</term> <note>In this context, likely "Philosophical Mercury," a specialized solvent used to break down the "body" of the metal.</note>,
  But not so much that the <term>Mercury</term> swims on top,
But in equal parts; then seal them up tightly <note>The original term "nipt up" refers to the practice of heating the neck of a glass flask and pinching it shut to create an airtight seal.</note> to save their "seed";
Then they are buried together within their "grave" <note>The alchemical vessel or furnace where the substances undergo a symbolic death and decomposition.</note>.

When you have it in this state and place, as I have said,
  Work in every way exactly as Nature worked at the beginning.
You need not be afraid of the <term>blacknes</term> <note>The "nigredo" stage, where the matter decays and turns black, signifying the start of the transformation.</note> that appears;
  It will eventually become <term>white</term> <note>The "albedo" stage, representing the purification of the matter and a successful step toward the final Goal.</note>, and then you are past the worst of the work,
  Unless you should break your glass and be cursed.

<summary>Edward Kelly provides practical instructions for grinding the alchemical "Stone" with Mercury on a porphyry slab and sealing it in a vessel. He describes the essential transition from the initial "blackness" of the matter to the successful "white" stage of purification.</summary>
<keywords>Edward Kelly, George Ripley, Philosopher's Stone, Mercury, Albedo, Nigredo, laboratory practice, porphyry, alchemical vessels</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s alchemical poem, following the instructions on how to grind the "Stone." Having prepared the matter, Kelly now describes the symbolic and physical progression through the elemental stages, specifically the transition from the black state of "putrefaction" to the white state of "purification."</meta>

Biographical Preface.
<page-num>51.</page-num>

But if through blackness you toward whiteness march,
Then it will be both white and soft as starch.

This very stage is called by many names—
  As <term>imbibition</term> <note>The process of "feeding" or saturating the dry matter with liquid (often called "mercurial water") until it absorbs it completely.</note>, feeding, and <term>sublimation</term> <note>A process where a substance is heated until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to symbolize the purification of the soul of the matter.</note>,
Climbing high mountains, and even <term>children’s games</term> <note>A common alchemical trope (Latin: *ludus puerorum*) suggesting that once the initial secret is mastered, the rest of the work is effortless and simple.</note>,
  And rightly it is termed <term>exaltation</term> <note>The raising of a substance to a higher "dignity" or potency, often through repeated processing.</note>,
  When all is nothing else but <term>circulation</term> <note>The continuous evaporation and condensation of a substance in a sealed vessel, mirroring the water cycle of nature.</note>
Of the four elements, whatever fools may chatter,
Which is performed by heat upon the form and matter.

Earth is the lowest element of all,
  Which, though black, is exalted into water;
Then no more earth, but water we it call,
  Although it seems a black and earthy matter,
  And in black dust all about it will scatter;
Yet when it has climbed as high as water,
Then it is truly said to be <term>sublimed</term>.

When this black mass again becomes white,
  Both inside and out, like snow, and shining fair,
Then this child, this wife, this heaven so bright,
  Is water-earth sublimed into air;
  When it reaches there, it further will prepare
Itself into the element of fire;
Then give God thanks for granting your desire.

This black and this white, we call <term>separation</term> <note>The alchemical stage where the pure is divided from the impure, or the spirit from the body.</note>,
  Which is not done by hand but by the elements;
It is no crude, chemical sublimation,
  But Nature’s true work, sharing the same substance;
  The white is called <term>natural conjunction</term> <note>The "marriage" of the refined masculine (sulfur) and feminine (mercury) principles of the Stone.</note>,
A secret and perfect joining, not coarse or gross,
Which brings great profit, while all other ways lead to loss.

<summary>Kelly describes the alchemical "circulation," detailing how the matter transitions through the four elements from Earth to Fire, eventually achieving the white stage of purification. This "natural conjunction" is presented as a divine and effortless process compared to the clumsy methods of "fools."</summary>
<keywords>Nigredo, Albedo, four elements, imbibition, sublimation, exaltation, circulation, spiritual alchemy, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s alchemical poem. Following the previous page’s description of the "white" stage of purification, Kelly now explains the "Multiplication" and "Projection" phases—the final steps where the alchemical Stone is used to transform base metals into gold and silver.</meta>

<page-num>i.</page-num>
# Edward Kelly :

When thrice you have turned this <term>wheel</term> <note>The "wheel" refers to the repeated cycle of distillation and condensation (circulation) required to increase the potency of the alchemical matter.</note> about,
  Feeding and working it as I have said,
Then will it flow like wax without doubt,
  Giving a <term>tincture</term> <note>The "tincture" is the concentrated essence of the Philosopher’s Stone, capable of "staining" or transforming the inner nature of base metals.</note> that will not fade,
  Abiding all trials that can be made;
If wisely you can project and keep free,
Both profit and credit to you it will be.

Your <term>medicine</term> <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" is another name for the Philosopher’s Stone, which was thought to "cure" base metals of their impurities to make them perfect gold.</note> fixed and perfectly flowing,
  White you must think will whiteness increase,
So red begets red, as seed in the sowing
  Begets its like, or as kind does in beasts,
  And fire must be the true maker of peace,
For white or red <term>ferment</term> <note>A small amount of pure gold or silver added to the Stone to "direct" its power toward creating that specific metal, similar to how yeast ferments dough.</note> your <term>medicine</term> augments,
  And perfectly tints and soon it melts.

That is to say, your <term>medicine</term> ended,
  If white, melt down silver and thereon 
  project it:
If red, melt down <term>gold</term> <note>original: "sol"</note>, for so it is intended,
  Like unto like, in no wise reject it,
  And out of the purest look you elect it:
<term>Medicine</term> one part upon <term>ferment</term> ten—
That one on one thousand of <term>tin</term> <note>original: "Jupiter"</note> then.

Your <term>tin</term> <note>original: "Jupiter"</note> standing red hot on the fire,
  So soon as your <term>medicine</term> upon him is cast,
Presently it stands as hard as a wire,
  For then he is fixed and melts by furnace blast,
  And of all your working this is the last;
Then let it by <term>test</term> <note>A refining process using high heat (cupellation) to prove the purity of the metal.</note> or <term>strong water</term> <note>Nitric acid, used by alchemists to dissolve silver and test the purity of gold.</note> be tried,
The best gold and silver no better shall bide.

<summary>This page describes the final "Projection" phase of the alchemical Great Work, where the finished Philosopher's Stone (the "medicine") is added to base metals like tin to transform them into silver or gold. Kelly emphasizes that the Stone must be "fermented" with pure precious metal to ensure the transformation is permanent and passes all purity tests.</summary>
<keywords>Projection, Tincture, Fermentation, Gold, Tin, Transmutation, Alchemy, Edward Kelly</keywords>

Crude mercury, when heated in a melting pot,
Soon hardens like tempered silver,
And takes its place upon the high throne of **Luna** <note>In the language of alchemy, "Luna" (the Moon) is the standard name for Silver.</note>.

Which many have seen, and I myself have performed,
And I value it highly, yet I give it for nothing.

## SIR EDWARD KELLY CONCERNING THE PHILOSOPHER'S STONE.

->*Written to his especially good friend, G. S. Gentleman.*<-

The heavenly vault contains within it the four natures—
Two are hidden, but the others are visible to the eye:
In which the seeds of all the lower bodies
Are kept most secret, yet sprout forth once a year;
And just as the earth and water are the creators,
So, for his part, dryness marks the end of his labor.

No flood is as great as that which flows constantly,
Nothing is more **fixed** <term>fixed</term> <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to fire.</note> than earth **digested** <term>digested</term> <note>Digestion refers to a process of slow, continuous heating in a sealed vessel, intended to mature the alchemical substance.</note> three times,
No wind is as fresh as when it serves one's will,
No profit is greater than to keep silent and be wise;
There is no better luck than to dry up air into dust,
For then you may finish your work, and satisfy your heart's desire.

<summary>This page concludes Edward Kelly's poetic instructions on the transmutation of mercury into silver and begins a new philosophical poem regarding the four elements and the "fixation" of volatile substances.</summary>

> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The learned **Sopheis** <term>Sopheis</term> <note>From the Greek "Sophoi," meaning the Wise Men or the true Alchemists.</note> will not reject you.
>
> If this my doctrine does not agree with your brain,
> Then I say nothing, though I have said too much;
> Truly, it was goodwill that moved me, not gain,

## SAINT DUNSTAN ON THE STONE OF THE PHILOSOPHERS.

### I.

Take as much of the best red transparent ore of gold as you can obtain, and drive its spirit from it through a <term>retort</term> <note>A retort is a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used in a laboratory for distilling substances or "driving off" vapors.</note>; this is the <term>Azoth</term> <note>Azoth was considered the "universal solvent" or the essential transformative agent in alchemy, often identified with the spirit of mercury.</note>.

...and the Vinegar <note>original: "Acetum"; in alchemy, this refers to a powerful acidic solvent, not merely household vinegar.</note> of the Philosophers, from its own proper ore <note>original: "minera"</note>, which fundamentally <note>original: "radically"; from the Latin 'radix' (root), meaning to act upon the very essence or root of the substance.</note> opens the **Gold** <term>Sol</term> <note>Sol (the Sun) is the alchemical name for gold.</note> that has been prepared.

### II.

Take the ore of **Copper** <term>Venus</term> or **Lead** <term>Saturn</term>, and drive their spirits out in a <term>retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long neck used for distilling or "driving off" vapors from a substance.</note>; each of these dissolves gold fundamentally, once they have been purified.

### III.

Take powdered ore of **Lead** <term>Saturn</term>, or common lead that has been roasted to ash <note>original: "calcined"</note>; extract its salt with **Vinegar** <term>Acetum</term> or its spirit <gloss>soul</gloss>; purify it in the best possible way, so that it becomes as transparent as crystal and as sweet as honey, and so that it melts in heat like wax but is brittle when cold. This is the tree of bitter fruits that has been cut down, onto which the branches of **Gold** <term>Sol</term> must be grafted <note>original: "inoculated"</note>.

### IV.

Take some of that earth which lies waste in the fields, found everywhere in marshy grounds, into which the celestial powers <note>original: "astrals"</note> cast their influences <note>original: "ejaculate their operations"</note>, being decorated with all kinds of colors so that it looks like a rainbow; extract from it its purest and most refined <note>original: "subtilest"</note> part. This is the universal solvent <term>menstruum</term> for everything; and it is all in all.

### V.

Take an equal amount of the ore of **Gold** <term>Sol</term> and **Quicksilver** <term>Mercury</term>; grind each very well; pour over it the spirit of mercury so that it stands three fingers deep. Dissolve it and let it warm <note>original: "digest"</note> in a gentle heat.

### VI.

Take of the best **vitriol** <note>A general term for sulfate minerals; "vitriol of Venus" specifically refers to copper sulfate.</note>, or of the **vitriol of Venus**; drive their spirits in a **retort** <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling or "driving off" vapors.</note>, white and red. With this red spirit, being **rectified** <note>Purified by repeated distillation.</note> and sweetened, you may ferment and soak the subtle **gold calx** <term>gold powder</term> <note>Gold that has been reduced to a fine powder through heating or chemical action.</note>, and with the white spirit you may dissolve it after it hath been purified.

### VII.

Take **quick Mercury** <term>liquid mercury</term>; purify and dissolve it so long in **alcolisated spirit of wine** <note>Highly concentrated or distilled alcohol (ethanol).</note>, until its impurity be separated from it, and it becomes its extreme, transparent, easy, fluid essence, like unto the **white gluten of the eagle** <note>A metaphorical term often referring to sal ammoniac or a volatile mercurial sublimate.</note>, and capable to receive the **blood of the Red Lion** <note>An alchemical metaphor for the "fixed" or "soul" element of gold or antimony, often a deep red liquid.</note>.

### VIII.

Extract the salt of the crude and white **calcined tartar** <note>Potassium carbonate produced by heating the sediment from wine barrels.</note>; purify and clarify it often, until it be as bright as a tear from the eye, and can be brought no higher; therewith you may sharpen its own **spirit of wine** <term>alcohol</term>, which dissolveth **Gold** <term>Sol</term> and **Silver** <term>Lune</term> <note>original: "Sol and Lune"; these are the Latin names for the Sun and Moon, the standard alchemical symbols for gold and silver.</note>.

### IX.

Take of the rank poisonous matter or stone, called **kerg swaden** <note>Likely a corruption of the German "Bergschwaden," referring to "mine-vapors" or toxic damp found in caves.</note>, **exuviæ** <note>Latin for "cast-off skins" or "husks."</note>, or husk of the metals; drive its spirit very circumspectly; receive it so that it may turn into water; it reduceth all metals to a **potableness** <term>drinkable form</term> <note>Alchemists sought to create "Aurum Potabile" (drinkable gold), believed to be a universal medicine.</note>.

### X.

Take of the **air or heavenly dew** <note>Dew was highly prized by alchemists as it was believed to contain the "Spirit of the World" or celestial energy.</note>, being well purified, ten parts, and of subtle **gold calx** <term>gold powder</term> one...

### XI.

Take the urine of a healthy man who has drunk only wine; produce from it, according to the art, the **salt of microcosm** <term>human salt</term> <note>Alchemical term for salts extracted from human urine, often containing phosphorus or ammonia.</note>; purify it very well, which so greatly sharpens the **spirit of wine** <note>Highly distilled alcohol or ethanol.</note> that it dissolves **Sol** <term>gold</term> <note>original: *Sol*; the Sun, which is the planetary and alchemical symbol for gold.</note> in a moment.

### XII.

Take the best gold ore; grind it into a very fine powder; seal it with **Hermes' seal** <note>An airtight seal made by melting the neck of a glass vessel; the origin of the term "hermetically sealed."</note>; place it in the **vaporous fire** <note>A steam bath or gentle heat (*balneum vaporis*).</note> for so long until you see it spring up into a white and red rose <note>Alchemical stages of progress were often described by colors; the "white" and "red" roses signify the nearing of the final perfection of the stone.</note>.

### XIII.

This last experiment he calls the **Light**. Take, in the name of the Lord, **Hungarian gold** <note>Gold from Hungary was renowned for its high quality and purity.</note>, which has been refined three times through **antimony** <note>A metalloid used to "devour" impurities in gold during smelting.</note> and has been **laminated** <note>Beaten or rolled into extremely thin sheets.</note> most thinly; take as much of it as you wish, and make an **amalgam** <note>A blend of mercury with another metal.</note> with **liquid mercury** <note>original: *quick Mercury*.</note>; then **calcine** <note>To reduce a substance to powder by means of heat.</note> it most finely with **flowers of sulphur** <term>sulfur powder</term> <note>A fine powder produced when sulfur is heated into a vapor and then cooled.</note> and burnt spirit of wine, repeating this until there remains a fine **gold calx** <term>gold powder</term> of a purple color. Take one part of it, and two parts of the above-mentioned red matter; grind it very well together for one hour on a warmed marble slab; then **cement** <note>To heat a metal in a bed of powdered chemicals to change its properties.</note> and calcine well by degrees for three hours in a **circular fire** <note>A furnace arrangement where the heat or flames surround the vessel.</note>. This work must be repeated three times; then pour onto it the best **rectified spirit** <note>Pure, highly concentrated alcohol.</note>, so that it stands over it three fingers deep;

set it in a gentle and warm **digestion** <note>The process of exposing a substance to steady, gentle heat over a long period to extract its essence.</note>, for six days to be extracted; then the **spirit of wine** <note>Highly distilled alcohol or ethanol.</note> will be tinged as deep as blood; pour off <note>The original "cant off" refers to decanting or pouring off a liquid without disturbing the sediment.</note> that tincture, and pour on another as long as it will tinge it; put all these tinged spirits of wine into a vial so that the fourth part only be filled, and seal it **hermetically** <note>An airtight seal made by melting the glass of the container's neck; named after Hermes Trismegistus.</note>; set it on the **vaporous fire** <note>A steam bath or gentle heat (*balneum vaporis*).</note> of the first degree; let it be of that heat as hot as the sun shines in July; let it stand thus for forty days—then you shall obtain your wish.

The author recommends this last experiment very highly, affirming upon his experimental practice that this **Drinkable Gold** <term>Aurum Potabile</term> <note>A legendary medicinal liquid produced from gold, believed by alchemists to have life-extending and restorative powers.</note> is the highest medicine next unto the universal <note>The "Universal Medicine" or Elixir, capable of curing any ailment.</note>, and, being taken in appropriated **vehicles** <note>Liquid substances, such as wine or water, used to administer a dose of medicine.</note>, cures all diseases without causing any pains at all.

*Item.*—With this **Drinkable Gold** <term>Aurum Potabile</term> is **antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid used extensively in alchemical medicine, often to induce purging or vomiting.</note> prepared, so that it purges only downward, and carries forth all ill **humours** <note>In pre-modern medicine, the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance determined health.</note> without molestation, and is called the **purging gold**.

It is prepared also by the aid of antimony into a **diaphoretic gold** <note>A preparation intended to induce sweating as a means of releasing toxins from the body.</note>, to expel by sweating all malignant humours; and the **Mercury of Life** <term>Mercurius Vitæ</term> <note>Original: *Mercurius Vitæ*. Despite the name, this was typically a preparation of antimony (antimony oxychloride) rather than quicksilver, prized for its medicinal effects.</note> is made also with this **Drinkable Gold** (if it be kept in a long digestion); their dose is according to the quality of the person.

<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

### III.

## THE ROSICRUCIANS AND DOCTOR DEE.

It is evident from the first section of this notice that Doctor Dee has been popularly regarded as an alchemist with about as much reason as he has also been regarded as a magician. No doubt he knew something of chemistry before he was acquainted with Kelly, and we have seen that he conducted a phenomenal series of experiments in **artificial lucidity** <note>This refers to scrying or crystal-gazing, where a medium attempts to see visions or spirits within a reflective surface.</note> through the mediation of his celebrated crystal; but he was not an alchemist on the one hand, nor a **necromancer** <note>A person who claims to communicate with the dead to predict the future or gain secret knowledge.</note> and a dealer with devils on the other. He was actually a learned mathematical philosopher, who was to some extent absorbed by the physics and metaphysics of the **Hermetic tradition** <note>An ancient philosophical and spiritual system based on the writings attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus, which profoundly influenced Renaissance science and mysticism.</note>. In particular, he wrote nothing on **Alchemy** <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The precursor to modern chemistry, alchemy focused on the transmutation of base metals into gold and the search for a universal panacea or "Elixir of Life."</note>, and it is necessary to accentuate this point, because a hypothesis has been recently put forward which it will not be unreasonable to dispose of in this place. It has been

<summary>This page argues that Doctor John Dee was a serious mathematical philosopher rather than a mere alchemist or magician, despite his famous interest in scrying and the Hermetic arts. It prepares to debunk recent theories about his work and his alleged lack of alchemical writings.</summary>
<keywords>John Dee, Edward Kelly, Rosicrucians, alchemy, scrying, Hermetic tradition, Renaissance philosophy, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the origins of the Rosicrucian movement, specifically addressing the theory that John Dee was its secret founder. It explores how historians often attempt to link famous Renaissance mystics to the creation of such enigmatic societies.</meta>

<page-num>lxi.</page-num>
<header>Biographical Preface.</header>

advanced that Doctor Dee was in reality the founder and head of the mysterious **Rosicrucian Fraternity** <note>A legendary secret society of sages and alchemists whose manifestos in the early 17th century claimed they possessed hidden knowledge for a "universal reformation" of the world.</note>, which publicly manifested its existence some twenty years after the death of Edward Kelly, but claimed to have been previously incorporated. Could the **philosopher of Mortlake** <note>A common nickname for Doctor John Dee, who resided and maintained his famous library in Mortlake, London.</note> establish his claim to this distinction, it is reasonably clear that his companion must divide with him the honours of having originated one of the most curious historical mysteries. Now, it is well known that, setting aside the imaginative persons who persuade themselves that the **Rosicrucians**, like the **Masonic Brotherhood** <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>A fraternal organization that historically emerged from stonemasons' guilds but is frequently linked in occult literature to ancient mystery traditions.</note>, can be traced to the period of the **Flood** <note>The biblical Great Flood of Noah; many esoteric traditions claimed an ancient lineage dating back to the patriarchs to establish their spiritual authority.</note>, and have disseminated the wild and unaccountable through all ages and in all countries—setting these aside, it is tolerably well known that investigators of the Rosicrucian mystery have cast about them on all sides for some one on whom they could **father it** <note>To "father" a mystery or movement means to attribute its authorship or creation to a specific person.</note>. ...proved a <term>Rosicrucian</term> <note>A member of a legendary secret brotherhood of mystics and alchemists whose manifestos in the early 1600s claimed they possessed secret knowledge to heal the world and master nature.</note>, it is fairly certain that <term>Kelly</term> <note>Edward Kelly (1555–1597), also known as Edward Talbot, was an English Renaissance occultist and self-declared alchemist.</note>, his inseparable companion, as well as his inspirer in <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transmutation of matter, particularly the attempt to turn base metals into gold.</note>, must also have been bonded with him in the same brotherhood; and <term>Kelly</term> as a <term>Rosicrucian</term>, connected with the founder of the order, would be undoubtedly of more interest than the "<term>skryer</term>" <note>A scryer or crystal-gazer; one who uses a reflective object, such as a crystal or mirror, to receive visions or communicate with spirits.</note> of <term>Doctor Dee</term> <note>John Dee (1527–1608), a famous mathematician, astronomer, and occultist who served as a consultant to Queen Elizabeth I.</note>, without prejudice to the philosopher of <term>Mortlake</term> <note>A reference to John Dee, who lived and kept his massive library in Mortlake, London.</note> or to the possessor of <term>Saint Dunstan's powder</term> <note>A red powder, supposedly discovered by Kelly at Glastonbury Abbey, which was believed to be the "Philosopher’s Stone" used for the transmutation of metals.</note>.

THOUGH I have already twice suffered chains and imprisonment in Bohemia <note>The author, Edward Kelly, was a famous English alchemist who served at the court of Emperor Rudolf II in Prague. He was imprisoned twice—first at Křivoklát Castle and later at Most—when he failed to produce the gold he had promised the Emperor.</note>, an indignity which has been offered to me in no other part of the world, yet my mind, remaining unbound, has all this time exercised itself in the study of that philosophy <note>In the 16th century, "philosophy" often referred specifically to "Natural Philosophy" or "Hermetic Philosophy," the precursor to modern science and alchemy.</note> which is despised only by the wicked and foolish, but is praised and admired by the wise. Nay, the saying that none but fools and lawyers <note>A common Renaissance-era trope suggesting that those focused on rigid human laws or ignorance were incapable of understanding the fluid, divine laws of nature.</note> hate and despise <term>Alchemy<note>The ancient art of transmuting base metals into gold and seeking the "Universal Medicine." The term derives from the Arabic 'al-kimiya'.</note></term> has passed into a proverb. Furthermore, as during the preceding three years I have used great labour, expense, and care in order to discover for your Majesty <note>Referring to the Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II (1552–1612), a dedicated, if somewhat obsessive, patron of the occult and alchemical arts.</note> that which might afford you much profit and pleasure, so during my imprisonment

->### Edward Kelly :<-

—a calamity which has befallen me through the action of your Majesty <note>Kelly is addressing the Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, who had imprisoned him in Bohemia (modern-day Czech Republic) after the alchemist failed to produce the gold he had promised.</note>— I am utterly incapable of remaining idle. Hence I have written a treatise, by means of which your imperial mind may be guided into all the truth of the more **ancient philosophy** <note>The "ancient philosophy" refers to the Hermetic tradition or alchemy, which practitioners believed was a secret wisdom passed down from antiquity.</note>, whence, as from a lofty eminence, it may contemplate and distinguish the fertile tracts from the barren and stony wilderness. But if my teaching displease you, know that you are still altogether wandering astray from the true scope and aim of this matter, and are utterly wasting your money, time, labour, and hope. A familiar acquaintance with the different branches of knowledge has taught me this one thing, that nothing is more ancient, excellent, or more desirable than truth, and whoever neglects it must pass his whole life in the shade.
