# The Stone of the Philosophers.

my own case. I venture to hope, however, that my life and character will so become known to posterity that I may be counted among those who have suffered much for the sake of truth. The full certainty of the present treatise time is powerless to abrogate <note>To "abrogate" means to repeal or treat as nonexistent; Kelly is claiming his scientific findings are eternal truths that time cannot erase.</note>. If your Majesty will deign to peruse it at your leisure, you will easily perceive that my mind is profoundly versed in this study.

(1) All genuine and judicious philosophers have traced back things to their **first principles** <note>In alchemy, "first principles" are the most basic, fundamental components of reality from which all complex matter is constructed.</note>, that is to say, those comprehended in the <term>threefold division of Nature<note>A traditional classification system used by early scientists to divide the world into three "kingdoms": Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral.</note></term>. The generation of animals they have attributed to a mingling of the male and female in sexual union ; that of vegetables to their own proper seed ; while as the principle of minerals they have assigned earth and <term>viscous water<note>original: "aqua viscosa." In alchemical theory, this refers to a thick, sticky moisture—often associated with a "mercurial" substance—that was believed to be the liquid base from which solid metals and minerals grow within the earth.</note></term>.

(2) All specific and individual things which fall under a certain class, obey the general laws and are referable to the first principles of the class to which they belong.

<summary>Writing from his imprisonment, Edward Kelly appeals to Emperor Rudolf II by asserting the permanence of his alchemical truths. He begins his formal treatise by defining the "first principles" of the three kingdoms of nature: animal, vegetable, and mineral.</summary>

<keywords>Edward Kelly, Rudolf II, alchemy, first principles, threefold division of Nature, viscous water, mineral generation, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s explanation of the "threefold division of Nature." Having established that all things come from first principles, he now details how specific categories of life and matter are generated and introduces the concept of the four elements and the "Chaos" that contains them.</meta>

->### Edward Kelly :<-

(3) Thus, every animal is the product of sexual union; every plant, of its proper seed; every mineral, of the mixture of its **generic earth** <term>generic earth<note>In alchemical theory, this refers to a fundamental "earthy" substance that serves as the essential base for all minerals and metals, distinct from the common dirt found in a garden.</note></term> and water.

(4) Hence, an unchangeable law of Nature regulates the **generation** <note>The process by which something is brought into existence or "born," whether it is a biological organism or a mineral growing in the earth.</note> of everything within the limits of its own particular **genus** <term>genus<note>From the Latin for "kind" or "origin." Kelly is arguing that nature operates within strict boundaries: animals produce animals, and minerals come from mineral foundations.</note></term>.

(5) It follows that, with reference to their origin, animals are generically distinct from vegetables and minerals; the same difference exists respectively between vegetables and minerals and the two other natural kingdoms.

(6) The common and universal matter of these three principles is called **Chaos**. <term>Chaos<note>Also known as "Materia Prima" or Prime Matter. In alchemy, this is not "disorder," but the original, formless substance created by God that contains the potential to become any physical thing.</note></term>

(7) **Chaos** contains within itself the four elements of all that is, viz. <note>An abbreviation for the Latin "videlicet," meaning "namely" or "that is to say."</note>, **fire**, **air**, **water**, and **earth**, by the mixture and motion of which the forms of all earthly things are impressed upon their subjects.

(8) These elements have four qualities: heat, coldness, humidity, dryness. The first inheres in fire, the second in <note>The text ends here, but in Aristotelian physics, "the second" (coldness) traditionally inheres in water or air depending on the specific elemental mapping being used.</note>

<summary>Kelly explains that all things are generated according to their specific "genus" and introduces Chaos as the primal matter containing the four elements and their qualities.</summary>

-># The Stone of the Philosophers.<-

water, the third in air, the fourth in earth.

(9) By means of these qualities, the elements act upon each other, and motion takes place. <note>In alchemical philosophy, "motion" refers not just to movement from place to place, but to any kind of change or transformation in a substance.</note>

(10) Elements either act upon each other, or are acted on, and are called either active or passive.

(11) Active elements are those which, in a compound <note>A "compound" here refers to any physical object or substance formed by a mixture of the four elements.</note>, impress upon the passive a certain specific character, according to the strength and extent of their motion. These are water and fire. <note>Kelly’s classification of Water and Fire as the "active" pair is a specific alchemical theory; in other traditions, Fire and Air are often seen as the active, "masculine" elements.</note>

(12) The passive elements—earth and air—are those which by their inactive qualities readily receive the impressions of the aforesaid active elements.

(13) The four elements are distinguished, not only by their activity and passivity, but also by the priority and posteriority <note>These terms refer to the order or rank of things—what comes first (priority) and what follows (posteriority) in the natural hierarchy.</note> of their motions.

(14) Priority and posteriority are here predicated <note>To "predicate" something is to state or assert a quality about a subject.</note> either with reference to the position of the whole sphere, or

***

-># The Stone of the Philosophers<-

**Edward Kelly:** 
the importance of the result or aim of the motion.

(15) In space, heavy objects tend downwards, and light objects upwards; those which are neither light nor heavy hold an intermediate position. <note>This reflects the traditional Aristotelian worldview where every object has a "natural place" it seeks to return to based on its composition.</note>

(16) In this way, even among the passive elements, earth holds a higher place than air, because it delights more in rest; for the less motion, the more passivity. <note>In this context, "higher place" refers to the ranking of its quality. Earth is considered the most "passive" because it is the most stable and resistant to change.</note>

(17) The excellence of result has reference to perfection and imperfection, the mature being more perfect than the immature. Now, maturity is altogether due to the heat of fire. Hence fire holds the highest place among active elements. <note>Alchemists viewed their work as a way to speed up the natural "ripening" or maturation of metals; fire was the essential tool used to drive this process toward perfection.</note>

(18) Among the passive elements, the first place belongs to that which is most passive, *i.e.*, which is most quickly and easily influenced. In a compound, earth is first passively affected, then air.

(19) Similarly, in every compound, the perfecting element acts last; for perfection is a transition from immaturity to maturity. <note>The "perfecting element" refers to the final application of heat or the influence of the "Stone" that completes the transformation of a substance into its most perfect form.</note>

<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s discourse on the "Stone of the Philosophers," following the previous discussion on the hierarchical nature of the four elements. Here, Kelly expands on the dualities of heat and cold, and the gendered classification of elements into active and passive principles.</meta>

-># The Stone of the Philosophers<-

(20) Maturity being caused by heat, cold is the cause of immaturity. <note>In the alchemical worldview, "maturity" is synonymous with perfection. Just as the sun ripens fruit, the alchemist uses controlled heat to "ripen" base metals into gold.</note>

(21) It is clear, then, that the elements, or remote first principles of animals, vegetables, and minerals, in <term>Chaos</term>, are susceptible of active movements in fire and water, and of passive movements in earth and air. <note>Chaos (original: *materia prima*) refers to the disorganized, primal state of matter before it is given specific form.</note> Water acts on earth, and <term>transmutes</term> it into its own nature; fire heats air, and also changes it into its own likeness. <note>"Transmutes" refers to the fundamental change of one substance into another.</note>

(22) The active elements may be called <term>male</term>, while the passive elements represent the <term>female principle</term>. <note>This gendered language was standard in alchemy to describe the relationship between forces: the "male" provides the spark or action, while the "female" provides the body or substance that receives the action.</note>

(23) Any compound belonging to any of these three kingdoms—animal, vegetable, mineral—is female in so far as it is earth and air, and male in so far as it is fire and water.

(24) Only that which has consistency is sensuously perceptible. Elementary fire and air, being naturally <term>subtle</term>, cannot be seen. <note>Subtle (original: *subtilis*) describes matter that is so refined or "thin" that it escapes human sight, often associated with the spirit or gas.</note>

(25) Only two elements, water and earth, are visible, and earth is called the hiding-place of fire, water the abode of air. <note>Kelly suggests that the more "spiritual" elements (Fire and Air) are nested within the "material" elements (Earth and Water), requiring the alchemist's art to draw them out.</note>

<summary>This page explains the division of the four elements into active (male) and passive (female) categories, noting that heat drives the process of maturation toward perfection. It also distinguishes between the visible elements of earth and water and the "subtle," invisible elements of fire and air that reside within them.</summary>

<keywords>four elements, maturity, Chaos, transmutation, male principle, female principle, three kingdoms, subtle matter, Edward Kelly</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s explanation of the gendered division of elements (male/active and female/passive). It moves from the abstract principles of elements into their practical application in the three kingdoms of nature: vegetable, animal, and mineral, focusing specifically on the "first matter" that constitutes each.</meta>

-># Edward Kelly: The Stone of the Philosophers<-

(26) In these two elements we have the broad law of limitation which divides the male from the female. <note>The "two elements" refers to the grouping of elements into active (fire/water) and passive (earth/air) pairs discussed on the previous page.</note>

(27) The <term>first matter</term> of vegetables is the water and earth hidden in its seed, there being more water than earth. <note>"First matter" (original: *materia prima*) is the fundamental, unformed substance from which a specific thing grows. "Vegetables" here refers to all plant life.</note>

(28) The first matter of animals is the mixture of the male and female sperm, which embodies more moisture than dryness.

(29) The first matter of minerals is a kind of <term>viscous water</term>, mingled with pure and impure earth. <note>"Viscous water" describes a thick, glue-like moisture that alchemists believed was the "mercury" or soul of the metal.</note>

(30) Impure earth is <term>combustible sulphur</term>, which hinders all fusion, and superficially matures the water joined to it, as we see in the minor minerals, <term>marcasite</term>, <term>magnesia</term>, <term>antimony</term>, etc. <note>In alchemy, "sulphur" was the principle of combustion and color. If the sulphur was "impure," it prevented the metal from melting properly. Marcasite (iron pyrites), magnesia (likely manganese ore), and antimony were seen as "immature" or "middle" minerals because they were not yet "perfect" metals like gold.</note>

(31) Pure earth is that which so unites the smallest parts of its aforesaid water that they cannot be separated by the fiercest fire, so that either both remain <term>fixed</term> or are <term>volatilized</term>. <note>"Fixed" refers to a substance that stays solid in the heat of a furnace; "volatilized" refers to a substance that rises as a vapor or spirit.</note>

(32) Of this viscous water and fusible earth, or sulphur, is composed... <note>The text breaks off here, leading into the specific composition of the "Stone" or the higher metals on the following page.</note>

**Terminology Reference Table**

| Term | Alchemical Meaning |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Vegetables** | All botanical life and plants. |
| **Viscous Water** | The metallic moisture or "mercury" of the minerals. |
| **Combustible Sulphur** | The oily, flammable principle that can "stain" or corrupt metals. |
| **Fixed** | Stable; resistant to change or evaporation by fire. |
| **Volatilized** | Transformed into a gas or spiritual state by heat. |

<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s discourse on the composition of metals. It completes the definition of "pure earth" from the previous page, establishing that the combination of pure earth and "quicksilver" (mercury) forms the basis of all metallic substances. The text then introduces the alchemical concept of "digestion" or ripening, where heat determines the maturity and perfection of a metal.</meta>

-># Edward Kelly: The Stone of the Philosophers<-

...from that which is called **quicksilver**, the <term>first matter</term> of the metals. <note>original: *materia prima*. In alchemy, this refers to the primordial, formless substance from which all physical things are created.</note>

(33) Metals are nothing but <term>Mercury</term> <term>digested</term> by different degrees of heat. <note>"Mercury" here refers to the philosophical principle of fluidity and "metallicity," while "digestion" is a technical term for the slow, constant application of heat, similar to how a body processes food or how a chick develops in an egg.</note>

(34) Different modifications of heat cause, in the metallic compound, either **maturity** or **immaturity**.

(35) The **mature** is that which has exactly attained all the activities and properties of fire. Such is **gold**. <note>Alchemists believed gold was "captured sunlight" or "solid fire," making it the only metal that could resist the highest temperatures of a furnace without being destroyed.</note>

(36) The **immature** is that which is dominated by the element of **water**, and is never acted on by fire. Such are lead, tin, copper, iron, and silver. <note>In this context, "never acted on" means these metals have not yet been fully transformed or "perfected" by the cosmic heat of the earth; they remain "watery" because they melt or oxidize more easily than gold.</note>

(37) Only one metal, viz., gold, is absolutely perfect and mature. Hence it is called the <term>perfect male body</term>. <note>original: *corpus masculinum perfectum*. Alchemical theory often used gendered language: gold (the Sun) was the active/male principle, while the "lesser" metals were seen as passive or developing.</note>

(38) The rest are immature and, therefore, imperfect.

(39) The limit of immaturity is the beginning of maturity; for the end of the first is the beginning of the last. <note>Kelly is describing a spectrum of evolution. He suggests that as a metal loses the last of its "immaturity," it simultaneously begins its existence as a "mature" substance.</note>

(40) **Silver** is less bounded by **aqueous immaturity** than the rest of the metals, though it may indeed be <note>original: *immaturitas aquosa*. Silver was considered the closest metal to gold, associated with the Moon. It was seen as nearly perfect but still containing too much "watery" or "cold" influence.</note>

<summary>Kelly explains that all metals are composed of mercury and "ripened" by heat. Gold is the only "perfect" and "mature" metal (the male body), while all others, including silver, are considered immature stages of metallic development.</summary>

(41) This condition is the reason why **silver** is everywhere called by the <term>Sages</term> the **perfect female body**. <note>original: *corpus foemineum*. In the symbolic marriage of alchemy, gold is the King (Sun/Male) and silver is the Queen (Moon/Female). Silver is "perfect" because it is pure, but "female" because it is less resistant to fire than gold.</note>

(42) All other metals differ only in the degree of their **imperfection**, according as they are more or less bounded by the said **immaturity**; nevertheless, all have a certain tendency towards perfection, though they lack the aforesaid **congealing vesture** of their earth. <note>Alchemists believed that nature always intends to make gold; other metals like lead or copper are simply "arrested" or "sick" versions of gold that failed to reach maturity in the earth's womb.</note>

(43) This **congealing force** is the effect of earthy **coldness**, balancing its own proper **humidity**, and causing <term>fixation</term> in the fluid matter. <note>"Fixation" is the process by which a volatile, liquid, or "watery" substance is made solid and stable enough to withstand the heat of a furnace without evaporating.</note>

(44) The **lesser metals** are <term>fusible</term> in a fierce fire, and therefore lack this perfect congealing force. <note>Base metals like tin or lead melt (fuse) at low temperatures or oxidize quickly. To the alchemist, this was a sign of their "weakness" or lack of internal fire.</note> If they become solid when they cool, this is due to the arrangement of their aforesaid earthy particles.

(45) According to the different ways in which this **viscous water** and... <note>original: *aqua viscosa*. This refers to the "thick" or "sticky" nature of the philosophical Mercury that forms the base of all metals.</note>

-># *The Stone of the Philosophers*<-

...pure earth are joined together, so as to produce <term>quicksilver</term> by coagulation, with the mediation of natural heat, we have different metals, some of which are called **perfect**, like **gold** and **silver**, while the rest are regarded as **imperfect**. <note>The alchemist explains that all metals are made of the same basic ingredients; their differences arise only from the "heat" and "purity" of the earth in which they grew. "Quicksilver" (mercury) acts as the liquid seed or "water" of the metals.</note>

(46) Whoever would imitate **Nature** in any particular operation must first be sure that he has the same matter, and, secondly, that this substance is acted on in a way similar to that of **Nature**. For **Nature rejoices in natural method**, and like purifies like. <note>original: *Natura natura laetatur*. This is a famous alchemical axiom. It suggests that if you want to make a metal, you must start with a metal. You cannot "breed" a horse from a stone, nor gold from a vegetable.</note>

(47) Hence they are mistaken who strive to elicit the **medicine** for the tinging of metals from animals or vegetables. The <term>tincture</term> and the metal tinged must belong to the same root or genus; and as it is the **imperfect metals** upon which the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> is to be projected, it follows that the powder of the Stone must be essentially <term>Mercury</term>. <note>The "tincture" is the concentrated essence of the Stone. "Projection" refers to the final act of the Great Work, where the alchemist throws a small amount of the Stone into a crucible of molten lead or copper to transform it into gold.</note> The **Stone** is the metallic matter which changes the forms of imperfect metals into gold, as we may learn from the first chapter of "The... <note>The text likely refers here to the "The Mirror of Alchemy" (*Speculum Alchemiae*), a seminal text in the medieval alchemical tradition.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Philosopher's Stone must be created from metallic substances—specifically Mercury—because Nature only works within the same "genus." It rejects the use of plants or animal products in alchemy, insisting that only a metallic "medicine" can transform base metals into gold.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 15, transitioning from the theoretical principle that "nature rejoices in nature" to specific textual proofs from alchemical authorities. It establishes the "Code of Truth"—the doctrine that all metals share a single primary substance: Mercury.</meta>

# Edward Kelly:
## The Code of Truth

> "The <term>Philosophical Stone</term> is the metallic matter converting the substances and forms of imperfect metals" ; and all Sages agree that it can have this effect only by being like them. <note>The "Philosophical Stone" (or Philosopher's Stone) was the ultimate goal of alchemy. Kelly argues here that if the Stone is to change a metal, it must itself be made of metallic material, following the logic that like only acts upon like.</note>

(48) That <term>Mercury</term> is the first matter of metals, I will attempt to prove by the sayings of some Sages. <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" is not just the liquid metal we know today, but a spiritual and physical "first principle" representing fluidity and the metallic essence.</note>

In the *<term>The Assembly of the Philosophers</term>* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*. This is one of the earliest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, presented as a debate among ancient thinkers.</note>, chapter i., we find the following words :

> "In the estimation of all Sages, <term>Mercury</term> is the first principle of all metals."

And a little further on :

> "As flesh is generated from coagulated blood, so **gold** is generated out of coagulated <term>Mercury</term>." <note>The alchemists frequently used biological metaphors—like blood and flesh—to explain how "dead" minerals could grow or transform into "perfect" gold.</note>

Again, towards the end of the chapter :

> "All pure and impure metallic bodies are <term>Mercury</term>, because they are generated from the same."

<term>Arnold</term> <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a renowned physician and alchemist who often wrote for the royal courts of Europe.</note> writes thus to the King of Aragon :

> "Know that the matter and sperm of all metals are <term>Mercury</term>, digested and thickened in the womb of the earth ; they are digested by <term>sulphur</term>— <note>Alchemists believed metals "grew" in the earth like embryos. Mercury was the "female" seed (sperm) and <term>sulphur</term> was the "male" seed or the heat that cooked it into a specific metal.</note>

<summary>Kelly cites ancient authorities and Arnold of Villanova to argue that Mercury is the fundamental "first matter" of all metals, comparing its growth in the earth to that of biological life.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical Stone, Mercury, Turba Philosophorum, gold, sulphur, Arnold of Villanova, transmutation, first matter</keywords>

-># Edward Kelly :<-

<meta>This page continues the technical argument for the common origin of metals. While the previous page introduced the concept of Mercury as the "sperm" or seed of all metals, this section provides authoritative proof from medieval scholars like Avicenna to show that metals can be reduced back to their original fluid state, proving their shared essence.</meta>

-># *The Stone of the Philosophers.*<-

...metals, perfect and imperfect, digested in the bowels of the earth by the heat of <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, sulphur was the active, "masculine" principle representing heat and fire, which "cooked" the raw matter of the earth into metal.</note>, the variety of metals being due to the diversity of their <term>sulphur</term>.”

We find in the same tract a similar rule: “All metals in the earth are generated in <term>Mercury</term> <note>Also known as quicksilver; alchemists believed it was the "mother" or primary material of all metals due to its fluid nature.</note>, and thus <term>Mercury</term> is the <term>first matter</term> <note>Original: *materia prima*. This is the fundamental, formless substance from which all physical things were thought to be created.</note> of metals.”

To these words <term>Avicenna</term> <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and chemistry were standard texts in European universities and alchemical studies for centuries.</note> signifies his assent in chapter iii: “As ice, which by heat is dissolved into water, is clearly generated out of water, so all metals may be resolved into <term>Mercury</term>, whence it is clear that they are generated out of it.”

This reasoning is confirmed by “<term>The Sounding of the Trumpet</term>” <note>Original title: *Clangor Buccinae*, a famous 16th-century collection of alchemical writings.</note>:
“Every passive body is reduced to its first matter by operations contrary to its nature; the first matter is <term>quicksilver</term>, being itself the oil of all liquid and ductile things.”

So also the third chapter of the “<term>Correction of Fools</term>” <note>Original title: *Correctio Fatuorum*, a treatise often attributed to the 14th-century alchemist Richardus Anglicus.</note>: “The nature of all fusible things is that of <term>Mercury</term> coagulated out of a vapour, or the heat of red or white incombustible <term>sulphur</term> <note>“Incombustible sulphur” refers to a highly purified, "fixed" state of the sulphur principle that can endure the fire without being consumed.</note>.”

<summary>This page cites historical authorities to argue that all metals share a single "first matter"—Mercury—and that their physical differences result from the specific type of Sulphur that "digests" them in the earth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the "mercury-sulphur" theory of metals, building on the previous page's citations of Avicenna and the "Clangor Buccinae." # Edward Kelly:

In chapter i. of the “**Art of Alchemy**” <note>This likely refers to one of Kelly's own treatises, such as "The Theatre of Terrestrial Astronomy," where he summarizes the foundational laws of the craft.</note> we read: “All Sages agree that the metals are generated from the vapour of <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, sulphur represents the active, masculine, and "combustible" principle that gives metals their color and hardness.</note> and <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Also known as Mercury; the fluid, feminine principle representing the "seed" or matter of all metals.</note>.”

Again, a passage in the *<term>Turba Philosophorum</term>* <note>Original Latin title meaning "The Assembly of the Philosophers." This is one of the oldest and most influential alchemical texts in the Latin West, framed as a transcript of a meeting of ancient Greek philosophers like Anaximander and Pythagoras.</note> runs thus: “It is certain that every subject derives from that into which it can be resolved. All metals may be resolved into <term>quicksilver</term>, hence they were once <term>quicksilver</term>.”

If it were worth while, I might adduce hundreds of other passages from the writings of the Sages, but as they would serve no good purpose, I will let these suffice.

Those persons make a great mistake who suppose that the thick water of <term>Antimony</term> <note>A brittle, semi-metallic substance. Many alchemists were obsessed with it because of its powerful purifying properties, but Kelly warns here that it is not the "first matter" of gold.</note>, or that viscous substance which is extracted from sublimed Mercury, or from Mercury and <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for the metal Tin. Alchemists used planetary names for the seven known metals: Sol (Gold), Luna (Silver), Mercury (Quicksilver), Venus (Copper), Mars (Iron), Jupiter (Tin), and Saturn (Lead).</note> dissolved together in a damp spot, can in any case be the <term>first substance</term> <note>Original: *materia prima*. The fundamental, underlying material that alchemists sought to isolate in order to transform it into the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of metals.

Antimony can never assume metallic qualities, because its water and moisture are not tempered with...

...dry, subtle, earth, and want, moreover, that <term>unctuosity</term> <note>A term used by alchemists to describe the "oily" or "fatty" quality of a substance that allows it to be flexible and melt without shattering.</note> which is characteristic of malleable metals. In the same way, those who destroy the natural composition of <term>Mercury</term> <note>Also known as quicksilver; the fluid, feminine principle believed to be the "seed" of all metals.</note>, in order to resolve it into a thick or limpid water, which they call the <term>first matter</term> <note>The *Prima Materia*, the original, chaotic substance required to start the Great Work of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of metals, fight against Nature in the dark, like **blinded gladiators** <note>This likely refers to the *andabatae*, Roman gladiators who wore helmets without eye-holes, fighting blindly.</note>.

As soon as Mercury loses its **specific form** <note>According to alchemical theory, the "form" is the spiritual blueprint that makes a substance what it is. If you chemically "kill" the Mercury too much, it loses the "form" of a metal and becomes useless for making gold.</note>, it becomes something else, which cannot thenceforth mingle with metals in their smallest parts, and is made void for the work of the Philosophers. Whoever is taken up with such childish experiments, should listen to the **Sage of Trevisa** <note>Bernard of Treviso (1406–1490), a renowned alchemist who spent his life and fortune searching for the Stone, eventually concluding that the process should be simpler and more natural.</note> in his *Transmutation of Metals*:

> “Who can find truth that destroys the humid nature of Mercury? Some foolish persons change its specific metallic arrangement, corrupt its natural humidity by dissolution, and dispropor...” <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page, likely meant to be "disproportion."</note>

<summary>Kelly argues that the "First Matter" is often hidden behind misleading names like Antimony and warns that over-processing Mercury destroys its essential metallic life, rendering it useless for alchemy.</summary>

<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

...tionate <note>This fragment likely completes the word "disproportionate" from the preceding page, referring to the act of upsetting the natural balance of the metal.</note> quicksilver from its original mineral quality, which wanted nothing but purification and simple <term>digestion</term> <note>A process of slow, gentle heating intended to mature a substance, mimicking the warmth of a womb or the earth.</note>. For seed in human and sensitive things is formed by Nature and not by art, but by art it is united and mixed. Seed needs no addition, and brooks no diminution. If it is to produce a new thing of the same genus, it must remain the very same thing that was formed by Nature. All teaching that changes <term>Mercury</term> <note>Also known as quicksilver. Kelly considers it the essential "mother" or "womb" of all metals.</note> is false and vain, for this is the original **sperm of metals**, and its moisture must not be dried up, for otherwise it will not dissolve. Too much fire will cause a morbid heat, like that of a fever, and change the passive into active elements, thus the balance of forces is destroyed, and the whole work marred. Yet these fools extract from the lesser minerals <term>corrosive waters</term> <note>Mineral acids, such as nitric acid (aqua fortis), which could dissolve metals but, in Kelly's view, "killed" their living spirit.</note>, into which they project the different species of metals, and thus corrode them.

<summary>Kelly argues that Mercury is the natural "seed" of metals and must be purified through gentle heating (digestion) rather than being destroyed by harsh acids or excessive fire. He warns that using corrosive substances like vitriol and alum ruins the metallic balance required for the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

# ->The Stone of the Philosophers.<-

...separating the <term>sulphur of Nature</term> <note>In alchemy, "sulphur" does not just refer to the chemical element, but to the active, fiery, and "masculine" principle that gives a metal its specific qualities and color.</note> from the metals.

Furthermore, as **Hermes** <note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy and the author of the Hermetic Corpus.</note> says, only <term>homogeneous substances</term> <note>Materials that share the same essential nature or "genus."</note> cohere, and only they can produce offspring after their own kind, *that is*, <note>original: "i.e." (id est)</note> if you want a <term>medicine</term> <note>The "Medicine" is a synonym for the Philosopher’s Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals into gold and restore human health.</note> which is to generate metals, its origin must be metallic, since "species are tinged by their genus," <note>original: "species a genere tingantur." This alchemical maxim suggests that the final product must share the same fundamental nature as its ingredients.</note> as the philosopher testifies.

In short, our whole <term>Magistery</term> <note>The masterwork or the complete technical process of creating the Stone.</note> consists in the union of the <term>male</term> and <term>female</term>, or <term>active</term> and <term>passive</term>, elements through the mediation of our <term>metallic water</term> <note>A specialized solvent, often associated with philosophical mercury, used to dissolve solid metals.</note> and a proper degree of heat. Now, the <term>male</term> and <term>female</term> are two metallic bodies, and this I will again prove by irrefragable quotations from the Sages <note>The "Sages" or "Philosophers" are the established historical authorities of alchemical lore.</note>:

> **Dantius** <note>Likely referring to John Dastin (c. 1293–1386), a famous English alchemist known for his correspondence with Pope John XXII.</note> bids us prepare the bodies and dissolve them.

> **Rhasis** <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist known in the West as Rhazes.</note>: "Change the bodies into water, and the water into earth: then all is done."

> **Galienus** <note>Galen of Pergamon, the influential Roman physician whose theories of humors and elements underpinned much of medieval alchemical theory.</note>: "Prepare the bodies, and purify them of the blackness in..."

<sig>D</sig> <note>The letter "D" at the bottom of the page is a "signature mark," used by early modern printers and bookbinders to ensure the pages were folded and gathered in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the creation of the Philosopher's Stone must begin with metallic substances, following the law that like produces like. It identifies the "Magistery" as a union of active (male) and passive (female) metallic principles through heat and dissolution.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur of Nature, Hermes Trismegistus, metallic medicine, male and female principles, Magistery, John Dastin, Rhazes, Galen, transmutation</keywords>

> ...which is <term>corruption</term> <note>In alchemy, "corruption" is not a bad thing; it refers to the necessary decay or breakdown of a substance so that it can be reborn in a purified state.</note>, till the white becomes white and red, then dissolve both, etc.”

### Calid (chapter i.) :
> “If you do not make the bodies <term>subtle</term> <note>To "subtilize" a substance meant to refine it into its most pure, vaporous, or finely divided form, often through repeated grinding or distillation.</note>, so that they may be impalpable to touch, you will not gain your end. If they have not been ground, repeat your operation, and see that they are ground and subtilized. If you do this, you will be directed to your desired goal.” <note>Calid refers to Khalid ibn Yazid, an Umayyad prince who was one of the earliest figures associated with the translation of alchemical texts into Arabic.</note>

### Aristotle:
> “Bodies cannot be changed except by reduction into their <term>first matter</term>.” <note>original: "materia prima." This is the formless, fundamental essence that alchemists believed sat beneath the physical appearance of all things. To change lead to gold, one first had to strip it back to this "first matter."</note>

### Calid (chapter v.) :
> “Similarly, the Sages have commanded us to dissolve the bodies so that heat adheres to their inmost parts; then we proceed to <term>coagulation</term> <note>The process of turning a liquid or dissolved substance back into a solid state.</note> after a second dissolution with a substance which most nearly approaches them.”

### Menabadus :
> “Make bodies not bodies, and incorporeal things bodies, for this is the whole process by which the <term>hidden virtue of Nature</term> <note>The "virtue" refers to the inherent power or secret essence within a substance that allows for its transformation.</note> is extracted.” <note>Menabadus is a character/philosopher cited in the "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts. This quote describes the core alchemical paradox: turning solids into liquids/vapors and vapors back into solids.</note>

<summary>This page compiles authoritative quotes from legendary alchemists like Calid and Aristotle to emphasize that metals must be completely broken down and "subtilized" into their primary essence before transformation can occur.</summary>

# The Stone of the Philosophers.

Ascanius <note>original: "Ascanius." This name appears frequently in alchemical florilegia (collections of excerpts), such as the *Rosarium Philosophorum*.</note> : "The conjunction of the two is like the union of husband and wife, from whose embrace results golden water."

"The Sounding of the Trumpet" <note>original: "Clangor Buccinae," an influential alchemical text first printed in the mid-16th century but circulating in manuscript earlier.</note> : "There is another <term>quicksilver</term> <note>In alchemy, "quicksilver" (mercury) often refers to the "Philosophical Mercury," a refined spiritual essence extracted from metals, rather than the common liquid metal.</note> and permanent <term>tincture</term> <note>A "tincture" is a potent substance capable of permeating and changing the nature and color of other materials, effectively transforming them into gold.</note> which is extracted from perfect bodies by dissolution, distillation, sublimation, and subtilization."

Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical "thrice-great" founder of alchemy, to whom many core doctrines were attributed.</note> : "Join the male to the female in their own proper humidity, because there is no birth without union of male and female."

Wed the man to the woman, and you have the whole <term>Magis-tery</term>."

Avicenna <note>original: "Ibn Sīnā," a Persian polymath. His works were central to the development of Latin alchemy.</note> : "Purify husband and wife separately, in order that they may unite more intimately; for if you do not purify them, they cannot love each other. **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian physician and philosopher. Although the historical Avicenna was skeptical of alchemy, many medieval treatises were written in his name (Pseudo-Avicenna) to grant them authority.</note>: “In order to obtain the red and the white Elixir <note>The 'White Elixir' was the stage of the work that turned metals into silver; the 'Red Elixir' was the final, perfected state that turned them into gold.</note>, the two bodies must be united. For though gold is the most fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" means stable and resistant to change or fire.</note> and perfect of the metals, yet if it be dissolved into its smallest parts, it becomes spiritual and volatile, like quicksilver, and that because of its heat. This tincture <note>A substance capable of "staining" or transforming the inner nature of a metal.</note>, which is without number <note>Meaning it has infinite power to transform.</note>, is called the hot male seed. But if silver be dissolved in warm water, it remains fixed as before, and has little or no tincture, yet it readily receives the tincture in a temperament of hot and cold, and is called the cold, dry, female seed. Gold or silver by themselves are not easily fusible, but a mixture of the two melts readily, as is well known to goldsmiths. Hence if our Stone did not contain both gold and silver, it would not be liquid, and would yield no medicine through any <term>magistery</term> <note>From the Latin *magisterium*, meaning "mastery." It refers to the specific technical process or the "Great Work" required to produce the Stone.</note>, nor tincture, for if it yielded tincture it would still have no tinging power.”

# *The Stone of the Philosophers.* 43

The Stone has body, soul, and spirit; the imperfect body is the body, the ferment is the soul, and the water is the spirit.” <note>Alchemists believed that matter, like humans, possessed a triple nature. To "perfect" a metal, one had to separate and then recombine these three aspects.</note>

**“The Way of Ways”** <note>original: "Semita Semitae." **“The Sounding of the Trumpet”** <note>original: "Buccina Tuae." In our <term>Stone</term> the ferment is like the soul, which gives life to the dead body...”

# Edward Kelly.

...never be weary of repeating these processes till they are quite perfect. For things saturated with water are thereby softened. The more you pound the substance, the more you soften it, and subtilize <note>To "subtilize" means to make a substance thinner, finer, or more spiritual by removing its heavy, "gross" physical qualities.</note> its gross parts, till they are thoroughly penetrated with the spirit and thus dissolved. For by pounding, roasting, and fire, the tough and viscous <note>Sticky or thick; in alchemy, viscosity was often associated with the "impure" sulfur that needed to be purged.</note> parts of bodies are separated.

The first is that of the **crude body** <note>The raw, unrefined mineral or metal as it is first taken from the earth.</note>.

The second is that of the **earth of the Sages** <note>Also called "philosophical earth," this is the purified solid residue that remains after the initial rounds of distillation and cleansing.</note>.

The third is that which takes place during the **augmentation** <note>The final stage where the potency or volume of the Philosopher’s Stone is increased.</note> of the substance. If you diligently consider all that I have said, this <term>Magistery</term> <note>The "Magistery" (from the Latin *magisterium*) refers to the completed Great Work or the specialized knowledge required to produce the Stone.</note> will become known to you. As for me, how much I have endured on account of this <term>Art</term> <note>The "Art" is the standard shorthand for Alchemy, considered both a physical craft and a spiritual discipline.</note>, history will reveal to future ages. <note>Kelly is likely referring to his turbulent career, which included legal troubles, accusations of fraud, and his eventual imprisonment by Emperor Rudolf II in Prague when he failed to produce the gold he had promised.</note>

# THE HUMID PATH.

---

**P**LATO has correctly defined philosophers as those who contemplate with wonder the marvelous works of Nature throughout every part of the created universe. These are the people who study the size, properties, movements, paths, and cycles of the heavens and their brilliant worlds—their rising and setting, their order of appearance, their speed, their irregularities and pauses, and their velocity. They also study the seeds, principles, dimensions, and inclinations of all <term>sublunar bodies</term><note>In the traditional Aristotelian worldview, "sublunar" refers to the Earth and its atmosphere below the moon, a realm where all things are subject to change, growth, and decay, unlike the "perfect" and unchanging heavens.</note>. Driven by a constant desire and thirst for knowledge, they are compelled not only to understand the mysteries and great <term>arcana</term><note>Latin for "secrets" or "mysteries"; in an alchemical context, it refers to the hidden, profound operations of nature.</note> of Nature with their minds, but also to imitate and even improve upon them. This can be very easily seen from the many—

<summary>This opening page defines the philosopher as a student of the entire cosmos, from the movements of the stars to the hidden principles of the earthly realm. It establishes the study of nature as a prerequisite for alchemical work.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, philosophy, Nature, astronomy, sublunar bodies, arcana, Edward Kelly, alchemy</keywords>

hieroglyphical writings, magical and mathematical mysteries, and all the other marks of the antiquity of philosophy. All these men firmly believed in the possibility of enjoying for many years a sound mind in a sound body, and this desirable result they considered as attainable only by the discovery of the <term>central substance</term><note>Also known as the "center of the world" or the "philosopher's stone," it is the theoretical substance that unites all opposing forces of nature into a perfect whole.</note> in which all the forces and virtues of Nature meet, following the <term>royal road</term><note>From the Latin *via regia*, this refers to the most direct, noble, and efficient path toward achieving the alchemical Great Work.</note> and philosophical method. They knew, indeed, that the mind is the most celestial, divine, pure, subtle, immortal, omniscient part of man, being receptive of God. But they also knew that the body, its dingy workshop of frail clay, obscures its move-

They <note>The "Sages" or ancient philosophers mentioned previously.</note> knew that some means was needed by which all excess might be trimmed away, all imperfections brought to maturity, all weak things strengthened, and all solid things made firm, so that the whole structure might rejoice in a guaranteed and continuous perfection. 

But in order to attain this end, they knew that they must have a deep and detailed acquaintance with the elements of the human body and of the universe generally. Before they could discover the cause of perfection, they had to first study the nature of the elements. The Sages saw that the instrument for reaching their goal was a thorough knowledge of the <term>physical arts and sciences</term><note>In the 16th century, these "arts" encompassed a blend of what we now call chemistry, physics, and natural philosophy.</note>.

After having conceived in their minds a **Divine idea** <note>In alchemical philosophy, this is the "Archetype"—the original, perfect blueprint of the cosmos as intended by God.</note> of the relations of the whole universe, they selected from among the rest a certain substance. ->Edward Kelly :<-

These facts were first brought out by <term>Hermes Trismegistus</term> <note>A legendary figure combining the attributes of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth; he was considered the "father" of all alchemical wisdom.</note> in his famous <term>Emerald Table</term> <note>Also known as the *Tabula Smaragdina*, this brief, cryptic text supposedly contained the secret of the "one substance" and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone. Its most famous line is "That which is below is like that which is above."</note>, and the truth of this assertion is borne out by the unanimous testimony of antiquity, and the consensus of the most illustrious men of all ages. 

That the aspiration of our <term>Art</term> <note>The "Great Art" (*Ars Magna*) or alchemy—the practice of refining base materials into something perfect.</note> is no Utopian dream, is proved by the innumerable and stupendous metamorphoses which <term>Nature</term> daily exhibits on every side. The <term>Sages</term> <note>The "wise ones" or ancient masters of alchemy who sought the secrets of life and matter.</note> have, indeed, purposely concealed their meaning under a veil of obscure words, but it is sufficiently clear from their writings that the substance of which they speak is not of a special, but a general kind <note>This refers to the "First Matter" (*Prima Materia*), which alchemists believed was a universal substance present in all things, rather than a single rare ingredient.</note>, and is therefore contained in animals, vegetables, and

It is the matter which the <term>Sages</term> <note>The "wise ones" or ancient masters of alchemy, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber, who supposedly discovered the secrets of transmutation.</note> have agreed to call <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the "spirit" or "fluid" principle of matter. If common Mercury be freed by <term>fixation</term> <note>The alchemical process of "fixing" a volatile or liquid substance so that it becomes solid and can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating.</note> from its crude, volatile, and watery superfluities, it may, with the aid of our <term>Art</term> <note>The "Great Art" (Ars Magna) of alchemy; the sacred craft of perfecting nature.</note>, attain to the purity and virtue of the substance of which we speak. And as this Mercury is the metallic basis and first substance, it may be found in all metals whatsoever. Other wise, learned, and sagacious men, who in perusing the books of the Sages failed to pay attention to this fact, have wasted both their time and their labour.

<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s explanation of alchemical theory, moving from the identity of the "Mercury of the Sages" on the previous page to the elemental mechanics of how minerals are formed within the earth.</meta>

## Edward Kelly :

Nothing contributes so much to a ready apprehension of our secret as a knowledge of our <term>first substance</term> <note>Original: *materia prima*. This is the fundamental, formless matter that alchemists believed could be transformed into any other substance, specifically the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, and after that of the distinctive species of <term>minera</term> <note>The specific mineral or ore that serves as the starting point for the alchemist's work.</note> which is the subject of investigation by the <term>Philosopher</term> <note>A term used to describe a master alchemist or "Sage" who understands the hidden laws of nature.</note>. You should learn that the earth is the mother of the elements, and that their arrangements and proportionate mixture are that which constitutes the difference between one species and another. 

Of these elements two, namely, **fire** and **water**, are active, while two, **earth** and **air**, are passive. <note>In the alchemical worldview, "active" elements provide the energy and movement, while "passive" elements provide the physical structure and container.</note> Fire and water strive to unite themselves to earth, but can do so only by means of the qualities which they have in common with it, i.e., in the case of fire, *dryness*, and, in the case of water, *coldness*. <note>This refers to the Aristotelian system where each element is defined by two qualities: Fire (hot/dry), Water (cold/wet), Air (hot/wet), and Earth (cold/dry).</note> So fire and water introduce themselves into earth by means of their dryness and coldness, and into air by means of their *heat* and *moisture*. Now, according as earth is more or less dry or cold, its centre will be occupied either by fire or water, while the other active element...

<summary>Edward Kelly explains the foundational alchemical theory that all minerals are created by the interaction of the four elements. He details how the "active" elements of fire and water penetrate the "passive" earth and air through shared qualities like dryness and coldness.</summary>

<keywords>Edward Kelly, first substance, elements, four elements, active and passive, Aristotelian qualities, mineral formation, alchemy theory</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Edward Kelly’s elemental theory, completing the thought from the previous page regarding how the arrangement of fire and water within the earth determines the nature of the resulting substance.</meta>

### The Humid Path.

will be confined to its circumference. <note>This concludes the sentence from the previous page: if fire occupies the center, the water is pushed to the outer edges of the "earth" or container.</note>

In the former case, the inborn dryness or heat of the fire being invisible and intangible, and residing, as it were, at the heart of the earth, will escape observation, but the humidity of the water, being more tangible and nearer to the surface, will be more easily noticed.

The surface of this compound will thus be watery, cold, and dry; and such is the substance which is commonly called <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Also known as Mercury. In alchemical theory, this is not just the liquid metal we know today, but a fundamental principle of fluidity and transformation.</note>. But it should be borne in mind that no account has been taken of the air that surrounds and, as it were, adheres to the earth in which fire and water are striving for the mastery. 

If the fire conquers the water, it will extend its operation to the air with which it has heat in common, and the exuberant strength of their united heat will subdue the humidity of the air, and impress upon it a new form of excessive dryness. The preponderance of fire will cause the colour of that element to tinge the whole substance, and thus we have that which is commonly called <note>The text ends here, but in alchemical tradition, the substance described—characterized by heat, dryness, and a red tinge—is Sulphur, the active "male" counterpart to Quicksilver.</note>

<summary>This page describes the elemental mechanics behind the formation of Quicksilver, explaining how the struggle between fire and water within the earth creates different mineral substances.</summary>
<keywords>quicksilver, mercury, elements, fire, water, alchemy, Edward Kelly, humid path, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the description of the three alchemical principles (Sulphur, Salt, and Mercury) begun on the previous page, specifically concluding the definition of Sulphur and detailing the formation of Salt.</meta>

## Edward Kelly :

**sulphur**. <note>This word completes the final sentence from the previous page: "the preponderance of fire will cause the colour of that element to tinge the whole substance, and thus we have that which is commonly called **sulphur**."</note> But if in the above case the water (in the earth) subdues the fire, it insinuates itself into the air by means of its humidity, and subdues to itself the heat of the air; now, as it is the property of cold to congeal, and this cold has been increased by that of the earth, there results a substance of icy whiteness which is called **salt**. 

These three (<term>Mercury</term> <note>Representing the principle of fluidity and volatility.</note>, <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Representing the principle of combustion and soul.</note>, <term>Salt</term> <note>Representing the principle of stability and the physical body.</note>) are necessarily the first substances of all minerals, and every mineral must be generated from one, or two, or all of them. But minerals do not consist of salt, sulphur, and mercury, as of parts which introduce the form, as some learned men have vainly supposed. <note>Kelly is arguing against a literal "recipe" view of alchemy; he believes these three are philosophical origins, not just ingredients mixed together like a cake.</note> For, in that case, such minerals would necessarily receive one or more of those forms in succession before they could be clothed with another. Rather they derive their being from one or more of these principles in different proportions as from their own proper source. For as the numbers 2, 3, and 4, are the foundation (of other numbers), though they them-

<summary>Kelly defines "Salt" as the third foundational principle of matter and argues that Mercury, Sulphur, and Salt are the essential "sources" of all minerals rather than mere physical ingredients.</summary>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three alchemical principles, specifically transitioning from the mathematical analogy of how they comprise different minerals to a distinction between "vulgar" and "philosophical" substances.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
->*The Humid Path.*<-

selves consist partly of units and partly of each other, as, for instance, 12 contains within itself 3 times 4, 4 times 3, 6 times 2, and 12 times 1, which are, nevertheless, all lost in its own proper name — so <term>Mercury</term>, <term>Sulphur</term>, and <term>Salt</term>, exist sometimes singly, sometimes in couples, and sometimes jointly, in mineral bodies. <note>Kelly uses this mathematical analogy to explain that although a metal might appear to be a single substance, it is composed of internal ratios of the three primary principles, much like the number 12 is composed of various factors.</note> And as 3, the fourth part of 12, consists of 3 units, or of 2 and 1 unit, while it is included in 4, which exceeds it by 1 unit, so some minerals which derive their motive force from a simple union of fire, water, and earth (which union, as aforesaid, constitutes Mercury), have no affinity with Sulphur or Salt, the perfection of which arises from the addition of air, the fourth element. <note>In the alchemical worldview, the "four elements" (fire, water, earth, air) combine to form the "three principles" (mercury, sulphur, salt). Here, Kelly suggests that air is the final ingredient required to achieve a more "perfect" or complex mineral state.</note> Here the question naturally arises whether Mercury contains Sulphur, and I say that, in the vulgar sense of that word — namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, in the sense of combustible sulphur — it does not. <note>"Vulgar" here means "common." --- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues Edward Kelly’s discourse on the composition of minerals. Having previously distinguished between "vulgar" (common) and "philosophical" substances, he now focuses on the internal relationship between Mercury and its stabilizing principle, Sulphur.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

its own <term>sulphur</term>, or naturally fixed earth, which is the cause of all <term>fixation</term>, a constituent and fundamental element of <term>Mercury</term>? <note>In alchemy, "fixation" is the process of making a volatile substance—one that evaporates or changes easily—stable and resistant to fire. Kelly argues that a specific type of "earth" inside Mercury provides this stability.</note> Nature has produced only two visible elements, the one active, the other passive, earth and water, in which the others, fire and air, which are naturally invisible and intangible, have their domicile and abode. <note>Kelly is working within the Aristotelian framework of the four elements. He suggests that while we can touch soil and water, the "higher" elements of fire and air are hidden forces contained within the physical ones.</note>

We can know only these outward and visible elements ; the bonds of the other elements can be loosed, and their presence ascertained, only by the ingenious contrivances of <term>art</term>. <note>Alchemists often referred to their practice simply as "The Art," implying that human skill and laboratory equipment are required to reveal the hidden secrets of nature that the naked eye cannot see.</note> Hence fire may be contained in a substance, even if it be not seen—and, to return to our enquiry, if in <term>quicksilver</term> by itself there is no combustible <term>sulphur</term>, but only a certain fixed earth, by which <term>Mercury</term> receives life, I am quite willing to call this fixed earth <term>sulphur</term>. <note>Kelly is resolving a common alchemical debate: does Mercury contain Sulphur? <summary>Kelly explains that while fire and air are invisible, they reside within earth and water and are revealed through alchemy. He identifies the stabilizing "fixed earth" within Mercury as its own specific form of Sulphur.</summary>

...the heavenly influences by which all earthly things are generated, moved, and disposed. ### The Humid Path

...does not tender its services to the multitude, but is the handmaiden of **Sages** <note>Referring to the "Sapiens" or the enlightened masters of the Hermetic tradition.</note>. 

Some think it is common **Mercury** <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemical texts, "common" mercury refers to the liquid metal quicksilver, whereas "philosophical" mercury is the specialized substance created through the work.</note> exposed to violent heat in a glass vessel; others say the **Mercury** must be very gently distilled in a glass vessel and rarefied. But all these persons are ignorant **philosophasters** <term>philosophasters</term> <note>A disparaging term for those who pretend to be philosophers or alchemists but lack true understanding and rely on literal, often dangerous, recipes.</note>. 

**Raymond** <note>Likely referring to Raymond Lully (Ramon Llull), a 13th-century philosopher whose name was frequently attached to influential alchemical treatises.</note> indeed describes a similar process, but he means something quite different, viz., that our **Mercury** is to be purified in a brilliant vessel, not to elicit water from it, but to free it by fire from its crudity, and to make it more readily soluble. 

Other methods, like the one suggested by the monk **Ravilascius** <note>A reference to an obscure medieval or early modern figure; alchemists often cited "bad" examples to contrast their own superior knowledge.</note>, not only betray gross ignorance, but are altogether absurd. Neither in one way nor the other can our water be elicited from common **Mercury**, or the mysteries of our **Magistery** <term>Magistery</term> <note>From the Latin 'magisterium,' denoting the entire secret process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> be unlocked. There is no **menstruum** <term>menstruum</term> <note>A solvent. The term comes from the Latin word for 'monthly,' comparing the chemical cycles in the flask to biological cycles.</note> which can so dissolve this **Mercury** that it shall retain its form; yet that is what our **Art** <term>Art</term> <note>The 'Sacred Art' or 'Spagyric Art,' meaning the practice of alchemy itself.</note> requires. Moreover, it seems absurd that the greater should be dominated by the less.

<summary>Kelly refutes common misconceptions about the use of ordinary mercury in alchemy, arguing that true philosophical water cannot be obtained through simple distillation or the methods of "philosophasters." <meta>This page continues Edward Kelly's argument regarding the hierarchy of planetary influences and the priority of Saturn (lead) over common Mercury in the preparation of the Philosopher's Stone. <page-num>86</page-num>

->**Edward Kelly :**<-

For instance, the **Moon** is passive with regard to the **Planets**, and yet is said to act on every one of those which are placed beneath it <note>In the traditional geocentric model of the universe, the Moon was the closest "sphere" to Earth; everything below it was the sublunary or earthly realm, subject to its change and decay.</note>. Should **Mercury**, then, which contains within itself the sphere of the **Moon**, be affected by the **Moon**? No; and much less can higher bodies be affected by **Mercury**, seeing that **Mercury** is rather affected by them. Even if common **Mercury** could be dissolved, - it could exercise its power only on the **Moon** which is contiguous to it. If we follow reason, it will tell us that the greater contains the less, and that this common **Mercury** has hitherto been accounted a slave and not a master. 

**Saturn**, on the other hand, includes within its circle the spheres of all the rest <note>Saturn was the outermost of the seven classical planets; therefore, its "sphere" was believed to encompass all the others, giving it a position of command and totality.</note>; by its virtue **lead** is produced, and it also has caused that **metallic water** to contain within it all essential properties. For not only can the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> be prepared from **lead**, as we have shewn, but **lead** itself may become the **Stone**. Its water will be a <term>menstruum</term> <note>Derived from the Latin *menstruus*; in alchemy, this refers to a specialized solvent capable of dissolving solid matter into its primary, liquid essence.</note> to all the rest, nor will the same thing that will dissolve

<summary>Kelly uses the hierarchy of celestial spheres to argue that Saturn (lead) is alchemically superior to Mercury, as it encompasses all other planetary influences. He concludes that lead, or its "metallic water," serves as the true universal solvent for creating the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

Solution is the action of any body, which, by certain laws of innate sympathy, assimilates anything of a lower class to its own essence. through the mediation of <term>Jupiter</term> <note>In alchemy, Jupiter represents the metal Tin.</note>, acts on <term>Saturn</term> <note>Saturn represents the metal Lead.</note>. But as the nature of <term>Mars</term> <note>Mars represents the metal Iron.</note> is most fitted to dissolve the <term>Sun</term> <note>The "Sun" refers to Gold, the most stable and perfect metal.</note>, <term>Saturn</term>, which has the same properties, may do the same, not, however, by virtue of its own proper nature, but because the nature of <term>Mars</term> is included in that of <term>Saturn</term>. This is to be understood of all the rest, after their kind. 

Hitherto we have explained the art of dissolving metallic bodies, by means of their own threefold <term>menstruum</term> <note>An alchemical solvent used to break down solid matter; the word is related to the "monthly" cycles of the moon, reflecting the time often required for these processes.</note>, into their proximate principles, viz., water and earth. Now, we will briefly describe the method of reducing metals into a more remote substance, viz., <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury, often considered the "first matter" or the spiritual essence shared by all metals.</note>. 

I will here take no notice of the venomous sayings of malicious astrologers; I shall have a word to address to them presently, when I shall also treat of the conjunctions and diameters of the planets, with their periodic and real <term>syzigiæ</term> <note>original: "syzigiæ". From the Greek word for "yoked together," this refers to the alignment of three celestial bodies in a straight line, such as during a new or full moon.</note>. 

Take <term>Venus</term> <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note>, or copper, the subject on which you desire to operate, and remember that you are trying to render visible a part which in its very nature is close to the centre. Ask

While the earth is kindled like a live coal, it should be stirred with an iron rod, so that all its parts may be perfectly <term>calcined</term>. Then take a fine sieve, finer than the earth, as soon as it has become cold, and purify it from the <term>crumbs of Saturn</term> <note>Lead-based impurities or dross that remain after the initial heating.</note>. [Take] gold in <term>Royal water</term> <note>original: "aqua regia"; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids, so named because it is the only acid that can dissolve "the king of metals," gold.</note>, produced through the <term>rectification</term> <note>The process of purifying a liquid through repeated distillation.</note> of <term>strong water</term> <note>original: "aqua fortis"; nitric acid.</note> with <term>burnt Sun</term> <note>original: "Sol ustus"; gold that has been calcined or reduced to a fine powder through high heat.</note>. Then the gold will be well purified, and this is the male and female substance <note>Alchemists believed that the "Great Work" required the union of masculine (gold/sulfur) and feminine (silver/mercury) principles.</note>, which must be united in this work with the water of <term>Antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-white metalloid; in this context, it refers to a liquid derivative used as a powerful transformative agent.</note>. This must be purified of its sediment twice in a hot bath, or by pouring it seven times through sand.

Digest by <term>twofold circulation</term> <note>A process where the liquid is vaporized and condensed repeatedly within the same vessel, often moving between two connected chambers.</note>, as you know, and it will become the true <term>Magistery</term> <note>A "Masterwork" or perfected substance capable of performing transmutations.</note> for transmuting <term>Mercury</term> <note>Quicksilver.</note> into gold.

## AN EASY WAY OF MAKING THE
## TINCTURE.

---

**T**AKE one ounce of gold, dissolve in <term>Royal Water</term> <note>original: "aqua regia"; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids. It was one of the few substances known to pre-modern chemists that could dissolve gold, the "King of Metals."</note>, steam off all <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>Latin for "strong water," referring to nitric acid.</note> by heat of ashes or sand, pour on this substance a good part of <term>Spirit of Saturn</term> <note>Usually referring to a distillate of lead, such as lead acetate or "vinegar of antimony," believed to hold cooling or transformative properties.</note>, and it will immediately receive a deep colouring.

Place the whole solution in a <term>retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long, curving neck used for distilling liquids by heating them and collecting the condensed vapor.</note>, and steam off the spirit with gentle heat. Pour this solution over the gold, as before, remove after two hours, and separate by gentle heat from the solution. 

The spirit is thus intensified and illuminated with the rays of the <term>sun</term> <note>In alchemy, the Sun (Sol) is the symbol and secret name for gold.</note>. The gold may then be melted and used for ordinary purposes, as it is not further serviceable in this work <note>The alchemist believes they have extracted the "soul" or "tincture" of the gold into the liquid, leaving the physical metal "dead" or ordinary.</note>. 

Place the spirit in a <term>pelican</term> <note>A specialized alchemical flask with two "arms" that curve from the neck back into the body. This allows the liquid to boil, rise as vapor, and flow back down, creating a continuous cycle of purification called "circulation."</note>, with one ounce of common **Mercury** seven times <term>sublimed</term> <note>A purification process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid. Doing this seven times was thought to achieve near-perfect purity.</note>, seal up <term>hermetically</term> <note>Airtight; the term comes from Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy. Alchemists usually sealed jars by melting the glass neck shut.</note> with the best wax, and place in a steam bath at a moderate heat; after five or six <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to "five or six days" or "weeks" of digestion.</note>

<summary>A practical recipe for creating an alchemical tincture by extracting the essence of gold into a lead-based solvent and combining it with highly purified mercury. The process emphasizes the use of specialized lab equipment like the pelican and retort to cycle and concentrate the "spirit" of the metal.</summary>

### A WAY OF MAKING DRINKABLE GOLD.

---

There are two kinds of <term>potable gold</term> <note>original: "Aurum Potabile." A legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid form, believed to cure all diseases and grant longevity.</note>. One is called the <term>Elixir</term> <note>The "philosopher's stone" in liquid form, capable of transmuting metals or healing the body.</note>, and is the stone liquefied into oil; the other is extracted from melted <term>calx of gold</term> <note>Gold that has been reduced to a fine powder or oxide through intense heat.</note> with the red <term>oil of Saturn</term> <note>In alchemy, Saturn is the symbol for lead. This "oil" refers to a substance derived from lead, often lead acetate.</note>. Nature develops what is good into what is better by the way of alteration. Gold which has not passed through alteration or physical solution has not been <term>educed</term> <note>Brought out or developed from a latent state.</note> into something better. <meta>This page continues the specific laboratory instructions for Edward Kelly’s "corporeal Mercury." ## *Edward Kelly:*

...passed through the cloth. This is our **magisterial, corporeal Mercury** <note>Also known as "Philosophical Mercury," this is a metal brought to a liquid, "living" state through alchemical art, making it fundamentally different from common quicksilver.</note> of signal perfection, and not the common kind. 

Its signs are these: 
*   That in its running it is not like simple Mercury.
*   That when placed on a body which is not **fixed** <note>To "fix" a substance means to make it stable in fire so that it no longer evaporates or escapes as vapor.</note> in the fire, so far as it spreads over that body it fixes it. 

Thus it fixes all bodies which were not fixed before, and in the above way you can get as much corporeal Mercury as you like. 

If you have made 20 oz. of this Mercury, take 5⅓ oz. of filings, pound small, add 10⅔ oz. of corporeal Mercury, form an **amalgam** <note>A soft, paste-like mixture created by blending mercury with another metal.</note> by pounding (making a soft paste). Divide the amalgam into three parts, put into a vessel, take one part of **calcined body** <note>A metal that has been reduced to a fine, crumbly powder through prolonged heating.</note>, and three parts of corporeal Mercury, mix well, add to the other two parts of amalgam, mix well, let it stand in the vessel in **hot ashes** <note>The "ash bath" provided a gentle, indirect heat, essential for the slow "digestion" or transformation of alchemical materials.</note> till the whole substance is changed into Mercury, and thus you can multiply this Mercury infinitely, so long as you have metal filings of any kind.

# THE THEATRE OF TERRESTRIAL ASTRONOMY.

**M**ANY books have been written
on the art of Alchemy,
which, by the multiplicity
of their allegories, riddles, and parables,
bewilder and confound all earnest stu-
dents; and the cause of this confusion
is the vast number and variety of names,
which all signify and do set forth one
and the same thing. <note>The author is addressing a common complaint in the 16th and 17th centuries: that alchemical "textbooks" were intentionally written in "decknamen" (cover-names) to keep secrets from the uninitiated, often making them impossible for even dedicated students to follow.</note> For this reason I
have resolved in my own mind to loosen
and untie all the difficult knots of the
ancient Sages. <note>The "Sages" refers to the legendary masters of alchemy, such as Hermes Trismegistus, Geber, or Rhazes.</note> I will speak first of the
inventors and restorers of this <term>Art</term>; <note>Art: In this context, "The Art" (Ars) always refers specifically to Alchemy, the "Great Art" of transformation.</note>
secondly, of the mutual conversion of
elements, and how through the pre-
dominance of one element the substance
of metals is generated; thirdly, I will
shew the affinity and homogeneity of
metals, procreated in the bowels of <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "the earth" on the following page.</note>

# Chapter the First.

## *Of the Inventors and Restorers of this Art.*

All Sages agree that the knowledge of <term>this Art</term> <note>The "Hermetic Art" or alchemy.</note> was first imparted to Adam by the Holy Spirit, and He prophesied, both before and after the Fall, that the world must be renewed, or, rather, purged with water. Therefore his successors erected two stone tables, on which they engraved a summary of all physical arts, in order that this <term>secret mystery</term> <note>original: "arcanum"; a profound secret of nature.</note> might become known to posterity. After the Flood, Noah found one of these tables at the foot of Mount Ararat. Others say that the knowledge of the Art was restored by Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure combining the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth; he was considered the primary founding father of the alchemical tradition.</note>, whose mind was a treasury of all arts and sciences; and <term>alchemists</term> <note>original: "chymici".</note> are still called sons of Hermes. Bernard of Trevisa <note>A 15th-century Italian alchemist known for his extensive travels and his treatise on the "Natural Philosophy of Metals."</note> states that the said Hermes came to the valley of Hebron, and there found seven stone <margin>tables</margin>
