## CHAPTER THE THIRD.

### *Of the Homogeneous Affinity of Metals generated in the bowels of the Earth; Harmony and Antipathy of Metallic Qualities.*

### *Metals consist of <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury is not just the liquid metal, but the "volatile" or spiritual principle of matter—the "Mother" of metals.</note> and <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur represents the "fixed" or fiery principle—the "Father" of metals—which gives them their color and hardness.</note>, and furnish us with the first substance of the <term>Elixir</term> <note>The Elixir, or Philosopher's Stone, was the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and curing all diseases.</note>.*

The various conversions of the elements which produce the first matter of metals have been now described. We must next treat of the nature of the said metals. It is clearer than daylight that there are <term>seven planets</term> <note>The Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, <term>seven days</term>, <term>seven metals</term> <note>Gold, Silver, Quicksilver, Copper, Iron, Tin, and Lead.</note>, and <term>seven operations</term> <note>The stages of the alchemical process, such as calcination, dissolution, and fermentation.</note>. The metals are called after the planets, because of their influence and their mutual relations. The mineral principles are living <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Sulphur</term>. From these are generated all metals and minerals, of which there are many species, possessing diverse natures, according to the purity and impurity of the <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Sulphur</term>, resulting in the purity or impurity of the generated <note>The text ends here, suggesting that the quality of the "parents" (Mercury and Sulphur) determines whether the resulting metal is "noble" like gold or "base" like lead.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the alchemical theory that all metals are born within the earth from two primary principles, Mercury and Sulphur, under the influence of the seven planets.</summary>

<keywords>Mercury-Sulphur theory, seven metals, planetary influence, alchemical principles, metallic generation, the Elixir</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the "Mercury-Sulphur theory" introduced on page 123. It provides a detailed chemical breakdown of the seven metals, attributed here to the famous English alchemist Edward Kelly, who was a close associate of the scholar John Dee.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>

<header>*Edward Kelly :*</header> <note>Edward Kelly (1555–1597) was a notorious English alchemist and spirit medium. This text attributes the specific definitions of metallic composition to his teachings.</note>

Metal. **Gold** is a perfect body, of pure, clear, red <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury is the fluid, "feminine" principle representing volatility and spirit.</note>, and pure, <term>fixed</term> <note>Fixation is the state where a substance can endure the heat of a furnace without evaporating or changing.</note>, red, incombustible <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur is the "masculine," fiery principle that provides color, hardness, and combustion.</note>. **Silver** is a pure body, nearly approaching perfection, of pure, clear, fixed white Mercury, and Sulphur of the same kind; it is a little wanting in fixation, colour, and weight. **Tin** is a pure, imperfect body, of pure, fixed and unfixed, clear, white Mercury outside, and red Mercury inside, with Sulphur of the same kind. **Lead** is an impure, imperfect body, of impure, unfixed, earthy, white, fetid Mercury and Sulphur outside, and red Mercury inside, with Sulphur of the same quality. **Copper** is an impure and imperfect body, of impure, unfixed, dirty, combustible, red Sulphur and Mercury. It is deficient in fixation, purity, and weight, while it abounds in impure colour and combustible <term>terrestreity</term> <note>Terrestreity refers to the "earthy" dross or coarse, unrefined matter that makes a metal base or "low."</note>. **Iron** is of impure, imperfect, excessively fixed, earthy, burning, white and red Sulphur and Mercury, is wanting in fusion, purity, and weight, abounding in fixed, impure Sulphur and combustible terrestreity.

<summary>This page defines the specific elemental compositions of the six main metals (Gold, Silver, Tin, Lead, Copper, and Iron) based on the varying purity and proportions of their Mercury and Sulphur components.</summary>

Nature transmutes the elements into <term>Mercury</term> <note>In this context, Mercury is the "feminine" philosophical principle of fluidity and spirit, rather than just the liquid metal.</note>, just as <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur represents the "masculine" principle of fire, action, and form.</note> transmutes the <term>first matter</term> <note>The *materia prima*, or the original, formless substance from which all things in the universe were created.</note>. The nature of all metals must be the same, because their first substance is the same, and Nature cannot develop anything out of a substance that is not in it. <note>This explains the alchemical belief in "transmutation": if all metals share a single root substance, one can theoretically be refined into another.</note>

## CHAPTER THE NINTH.

### *The Conjunction of Sun and Moon.*

The ancient philosophers have enumerated several kinds of <term>conjunction</term> <note>The chemical and symbolic "marriage" or union of opposing principles, such as male and female or spirit and body.</note>, but to avoid a vain prolixity I will affirm, upon the testimony of Marsilio Ficino <note>original: "Marsilius Ficinus"; a 15th-century Italian philosopher whose work bridged Neoplatonism and Hermetic alchemy.</note>, that conjunction is the union of separate qualities, or an equation of principles, namely: <term>Mercury</term> <note>The volatile, fluid, or "feminine" principle of matter.</note> and <term>Sulphur</term> <note>The active, fiery, or "masculine" principle of matter.</note>, **Sun** and **Moon**, agent and patient, matter and form. When the virgin, or feminine, earth is thoroughly purified and purged from all superfluity, you must give it a husband meet for it; for

When the male and the female are joined together by means of the sperm, a generation must take place in the <term>menstruum</term> <note>original: "menstruum"; in alchemy, this refers to a solvent or a base substance that acts as a "womb" to dissolve and nurture the materials, named by analogy to the biological environment.</note>. The substance of <term>Mercury</term> <note>The volatile, fluid, or "feminine" principle of matter.</note> is known to the <term>Sages</term> <note>The ancient masters of the alchemical art, often referred to as the "Philosophers."</note> as the earth and matter in which the <term>Sulphur of Nature</term> <note>The active, fiery, or "masculine" principle that provides the "seed" for the transformation.</note> is sown, that it may there undergo <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The process of breaking down and decaying, which alchemists believed was necessary for new life and purity to emerge.</note>, the earth being its womb. Here the female seed awaits that of the male, by means of which they are inseparably united, the one being hot and dry, and the other cold and moist; the heat and dryness of the male are tempered with the cold and moisture of the female, and, in due time, the matter will assume a specific form. For all action tends to the production of a form, being, as it is, an efficient principle.

Our substance, according to the

black eyes <note>This phrase completes the final sentence from page 139: "Our substance... has a red head, white feet, and black eyes."</note>. The beginning of our work is the **Black Raven** <note>A symbol for the "Nigredo" or blackening stage, representing the death and decomposition of the old matter.</note>, which, like all things that are to grow and receive life, must first putrefy <note>In alchemical theory, life cannot emerge until the old form is completely broken down through rot or decay.</note>. For putrefaction is a necessary condition of solution, as solution is of birth and regeneration. This putrefaction is not impure, but a commixtion <note>A thorough blending or "mingling" of elements at their most basic level.</note>, in their smallest parts, of earth with water, and water with earth, till the whole body becomes one. The red male must be digested in union with his white wife, till both become dry—for otherwise no colours will appear. When the dry principle acts on the moist, flowers of all the colours of a **Peacock’s Tail** <note>Original Latin: *Cauda Pavonis*. As the moisture is gradually dried up, these shifting colours give place to a settled whiteness.

This white tincture, or <term>elixir</term> — <note>A refined substance capable of transmuting metals or healing the body.</note>, is also called the <term>Virgin's milk</term> — <note>A poetic term for the white, nourishing liquid observed during the Albedo stage.</note>, the everlasting water, and water of life, because it is as brilliant as white marble; it is also called the <term>White Queen</term> — <note>The symbol for the lunar stage of the work, which produces silver.</note>, who by increasing the fire becomes the <term>Mighty King</term> — <note>The symbol for the solar stage or "Red Work" (Rubedo), which produces gold.</note>, the white transforming into yellow and saffron, and at last into a deep ruby colour.

## CHAPTER THE THIRTEENTH.

### *Of the Perfect Red Elixir.*

Xiphilinus <note>likely Joannes Xiphilinus, an 11th-century Byzantine historian whose writings were often cited in alchemical florilegia to provide historical or philosophical authority.</note> and the rest of the philosophers agree in this, that the white colour must precede the red. As

<summary>This page describes an allegorical emblem of a King and kneeling planets, signaling the completion of the White Work and the transition to the final Red stage (Rubedo). It begins Chapter 13, which focuses on the "Perfect Red Elixir."</summary>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 143, which introduced Chapter 13 and the transition from the "White King" (Albedo) to the preparations for the "Perfect Red Elixir" (Rubedo). It expands on the philosophical necessity of following a specific sequence of colors during the chemical transformation.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>Edward Kelly :</header>

You can have no red colour where the substance has not first been white, just as the black cannot become orange unless it first becomes white. In like manner, the <term>Rosary</term> <note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), one of the most famous alchemical florilegia, first printed in 1550.</note> says that nothing can become gold that has not first been silver. He who knows how to convert gold into silver also knows how to convert silver into gold. Gold, to become silver, must first be <term>corrupted</term> <note>In alchemy, "corruption" or *putrefactio* refers to the essential breakdown of a substance into its base components, often represented by the color black, before it can be reconstituted into a higher form.</note> and made black, and there is no method of becoming yellow except by way of white; in the same way the white must become red by way of yellow. Heat, acting on moisture, causes blackness; acting on dryness—especially if it be continued carefully and unceasingly—there is developed true whiteness. Out of white comes yellow, and out of yellow a permanent and <term>tinging</term> <note>A "tinging" substance is one capable of imparting its color and quality to other metals, essentially a successful Tincture or Philosopher's Stone.</note> ruby colour.

# The Theatre of Terrestrial Astronomy
<note>original: *Theatrum Astronomiae Terrestris*. This title suggests that alchemy is a form of "earthly" astronomy, where the alchemist observes the growth and movement of "planets" (metals) within the earth and the laboratory.</note>

->An old man in a tunic stands by a <term>furnace</term> <note>Often referred to as the *Athanor* or "painless furnace," this device was engineered to maintain a constant, steady heat for the months-long duration of the Great Work.</note>, one tower of which is open, and in the <term>urinal</term> <note>In alchemical texts, a "urinal" (or *urinalis*) refers to a glass flask or vessel with a rounded bottom, so named for its shape, used for the "digestion" or slow heating of chemical substances.</note> of the other is a **purple Sun**.<-

<note>The "purple Sun" (*Sol Purpureus*) is a specific symbol for the final stage of alchemy, known as the *Rubedo* or Red Work. While red is the standard color of the finished Philosopher's Stone, purple represents the highest possible degree of purity and "tinging" power.</note>

### Vocabulary

| Term | Significance |
| :--- | :--- |
| **The Theatre of Terrestrial Astronomy** | The title of the work, suggesting that alchemy is the study of "stars" or celestial forces as they manifest within the Earth and its minerals. |
| **Red** (*Rubeus*) | The highest color in alchemy, signifying the completion of the Philosopher's Stone. |
| **Yellow** (*Flavus*) | A transitional phase between the "white" stage (silver) and the "red" stage (gold). |

| **Sun and Moon** | The two primary "Luminaries"; in this context, they represent the union of opposites (male/female, hot/cold, fixed/volatile). |
| **Quadrangle** | Symbolizes the material world and the four elements that the alchemist seeks to balance and transcend. |

<meta>This page serves as a title or promotional page for a modern English edition of a historical work, following a blank page in the manuscript. ### PRICE TWO GUINEAS. <note>A guinea was a gold coin worth 21 shillings. In the late 19th century, two guineas was an expensive price for a book, roughly equivalent to several hundred dollars today.</note>

#### In two volumes, small quarto, cloth extra, gilt. <note>Quarto refers to the book size, where a sheet of paper is folded twice to make four leaves.</note>

# The Hermetic Museum

->Restored and enlarged, most faithfully instructing all the Disciples of the <term>Sopho-Spagyric Art</term> <note>A term combining the Greek 'sophos' (wise) with 'spagyric' (the alchemical process of separating and recombining elements to achieve purity).</note>, how that Greatest and Truest Medicine of the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>The ultimate goal of alchemy: a substance believed to transmute base metals into gold and serve as a universal medicine.</note> may be found and held.<-

->Now first done into English from the rare Latin Original, published at Frankfort in the year 1678. The illustrations reproduced in facsimile by a photographic process.<-

This curious storehouse of <term>Hermetic Science</term> <note>Knowledge based on the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, concerning the spiritual and physical laws of the universe.</note> comprises twenty-two choice treatises on the Mysteries of <term>Alchemy</term>, and the composition of the Medicine of the Philosopher, namely .—

| | |
|---|---|
| The Golden Treatise concerning the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term>. | The Ordinal of <term>Alchemy</term>. |
| The Golden Age come back. | The Testament of John Cremer, sometime Abbot of Westminster. <note>A legendary figure in English alchemy; his presence in historical records as an Abbot is debated.</note> |
| The Sophic Hydrolith, or Water Stone of the Wise. | The New Light of <term>Alchemy</term>. |
| The Demonstration of Nature. | The Sulphur of the Philosophers. |
| A Philosophical Summary. | An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King. |
| The Path of the only Truth. | A Subtle Allegory concerning the Secrets of Chemistry. |
| The Glory of the World, or Table of Paradise. | The Metamorphosis of Metals. |
| The Generation of Metals. | A Short Guide to the Celestial Ruby. |
| The Book of Alze. | The Fount of Chemical Truth. |
| Figures and Emblems concerning the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term>. | The Golden Calf. |
| The Practice and Keys of Basil Valentine. <note>A pseudonymous 15th-century author, traditionally described as a Benedictine monk, whose works were foundational to later alchemy.</note> | The All-Wise Doorkeeper. |

While affording to the modern student of <term>Hermetic Doctrines</term> an unique opportunity of acquiring in English a representative collection of the chief alchemical writers, this edition of THE HERMETIC MUSEUM claims consideration at the hands of the historian and archæologist as a contribution <note>Likely a printer's error for 'contribution'.</note> of real value to the early history of chemistry. The translation is the work of a gentleman who has had a life-long acquaintance with alchemical literature, and has been subjected to careful revision by another expert in <term>Hermetic Antiquities</term>.

*Note: <note>original: "N.B." (Nota Bene)</note>—This Edition is limited to 250 copies, numbered and signed.*

# The Pearl of Great Price

## A NEW TREATISE CONCERNING THE TREASURE AND MOST PRECIOUS PHILOSOPHER’S STONE <note>The legendary substance believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold and acting as a universal panacea.</note> ;

->Or the Method and Procedure of this Divine Art; being observations drawn from the works of Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemical writer.</note>, Raymond <note>Raymond Lully (Ramon Llull, c. 1232–1316), a mystic and philosopher to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note>, Rhasis <note>Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath who made significant early contributions to chemistry.</note>, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican scholar known for his work in the natural sciences.</note>, and Michael Scot <note>Michael Scot (c. 1175–1232), a scholar and translator known in legend as a powerful magician.</note>. Now first published by James Lacinius the Calabrian, with a comprehensive Index.<-

->Translated into English from the highly valued edition by Aldus <note>The Aldine Press in Venice, renowned for its scholarship and beautiful typography.</note>, which appeared under the authority of Pope Paul III and the Senate of Venice in 1546.<-

---

The *New Pearl of Great Price* <note>original: "Pretiosa Margarita Novella"</note> is believed to be a “faithful summary” of a work titled the *Precious Pearl* <note>original: "Margarita Pretiosa"</note>, which seems to have circulated in manuscript form in Italy during the first half of the fourteenth century, though it was apparently never printed. It was written by Pietro Bono, who is highly regarded as an expert in the art of <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The ancient practice focusing on the transmutation of matter and the pursuit of spiritual perfection.</note>; and the current translation, which has been thoroughly revised, is edited with an introductory analysis of the various <term>Hermetic</term> <note>Referring to the philosophical and magical traditions attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> books attributed to this author. The *New Pearl of Great Price* is particularly significant as one of the very first books on <term>Alchemy</term> ever to be printed.

# The Pearl of Great Price

## A NEW TREATISE CONCERNING THE TREASURE AND MOST PRECIOUS PHILOSOPHER’S STONE <note>The "Philosopher’s Stone" was the legendary goal of alchemy, a substance believed to be capable of turning lead into gold and providing a "universal medicine" for healing and longevity.</note> ;

->Or the Method and Procedure of this Divine Art; being observations drawn from the works of Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemical writer who served several kings and popes.</note>, Raymond <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1316), a mystic and philosopher. While he likely didn't write alchemical texts himself, many famous works were attributed to him to give them authority.</note>, Rhasis <note>Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and physician who wrote some of the earliest systematic guides to chemical classification and laboratory equipment.</note>, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a celebrated Dominican scholar and teacher of Thomas Aquinas, renowned for his extensive knowledge of the natural sciences.</note>, and Michael Scot <note>Michael Scot (c. 1175–1232), a scholar and translator at the court of Frederick II, who became a legendary figure associated with magic and the occult.</note>. Now first published by James Lacinius <note>original: "James Lacinius." This refers to Janus Lacinius, a 16th-century monk from Calabria who edited the original collection.</note> the Calabrian, with a comprehensive Index.<-
